Critical CoHAs, vertex coalgebras and Deformed Drinfeld coproducts
Shivang Jindal, Šarūnas Kaubrys, Alexei Latyntsev

Abstract




Abstract
: We construct a vertex coproduct on the Kontsevich–Soibelman cohomological Hall algebra (CoHA) of a quiver
with potential, following Joyce
[
29
]
. We show it forms a vertex bialgebra. By applying a vertex algebraic analogue of Majid–Radford bosonisation, we form an extension of the CoHA of quivers with potential which incorporates a Cartan part. In the case of ADE quivers our vertex coproduct recovers Drinfeld’s deformed coproduct on the Yangian. We compare the vertex coproduct with a localised
coproduct defined by Davison and with the construction of Dotsenko–Mozgovoy when the potential is trivial. Our construction gives a new proof of the cohomological integrality theorem for symmetric quivers with trivial potential.

0.
Introduction

Using natural geometric structures on moduli spaces of objects
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
in abelian or derived categories
\mathcal{C}
, Joyce
[
30
,
29
]
has constructed a structure of a vertex algebra on the homology
\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}})
or equivalently a vertex coalgebra on the cohomology. This structure has already found several applications in enumerative geometry such as
[
3
,
25
,
6
]
and
[
36
]
in the K-theory setting. However, most enumerative geometry is interested instead in the
vanishing cycle cohomology
or Borel–Moore homology of such stacks. In this paper we fill this gap, defining a vertex coalgebra on critical Cohomological Hall algebras of quivers with potential. This gives a new geometric representation theory interpretation of these vertex coproducts, matching them with known (Drinfeld) coproducts on quantum groups.

Let
Q
be a quiver with potential
W
. We study two structures on the following vector space

(1)

\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}\ =\ \bigoplus_{d\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d},\varphi_{\textup{Tr}W_{d}}\textbf{{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}}[\dim\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}]),

where
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
is the moduli stack of quiver representations and
\varphi_{\textup{Tr}W,d}\textbf{{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}}[\dim\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}]
is the vanishing cycles perverse sheaf.
The first algebraic structure is the Kontsevich-Soibelman Cohomological Hall algebra (CoHA)
[
32
]
.
The second is the new vertex coproduct, an algebraic structure generalising work of Joyce
[
29
]
in the case of
W=0
, and analogous to Liu’s vertex coproduct on critical K-theory
[
37
]
for tripled quivers.

We show these structures are compatible: they form a (vertex)
bialgebra
in an ambient (meromorphic) braided category of modules over a tautological subring of cohomology. These types of results go back to Ringel and Green
[
24
,
47
]
who also showed that Hall algebras are quantum groups. Analogously, we show that for triples of Dynkin quivers associated to simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we have an isomorphism of (vertex) bialgebras exchanging our vertex coproduct with the Drinfeld coproduct on Yangians:
1
1

1

The superscripts relate to working equivariantly with respect to a one-dimensional torus with
T_{\hbar}
with
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T_{\hbar})=\textbf{{C}}[\hbar]
, adding a sign correction
\chi
to the CoHA product, and bosonisation, respectively.

(2)

\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}^{T_{\hbar},\chi,\textup{ext}}\ \simeq\ Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{\geq 0}.

The isomorphism as algebras is due to Yang–Zhao
[
54
]
, in which the authors also construct a coproduct, which they show is equivalent to the Drinfeld coproduct.

Being a vertex bialgebra allows us to apply
vertex Majid–Radford bosonisation
to extend CoHAs uniformly, i.e. add a Cartan piece, and in the Dynkin case this formula for its coproduct induces (
2
). If
W=0
, we show that the Joyce vertex algebra is a universal chiral envelope and show it is isomorphic to the construction of
[
15
]
.

0.1.
Cohomological Hall product and Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct

We now give a more detailed introduction to the algebraic structures we study in this paper.

0.1.1.

All algebraic structures on
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}
will be mirrored by and constructed from geometric structures on the moduli stack
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
of representations of the quiver
Q
.

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{SES}_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{SES}_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

On the left we have the stack parametrising short exact sequences of representations of
Q
, with maps
p
and
q
forgetting all but the middle and outer terms. On the right we have the map
\oplus
taking direct sum of representations, and only commutes if we replace
q
with its section
s
taking a pair of representations to the trivial direct sum extension.

In fact, the above diagrams are equivariant for a certain class of actions by a torus
T
leaving the potential of the quiver invariant, and we usually consider a
T
-equivariant version
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
of the vanishing cycles cohomology in (
1
), and
\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}
the stack quotient by
T
.

The Kontsevich–Soibelman CoHA product
[
32
]
on critical cohomology is defined by pulling back by
q
and then pushing forward by
p
. The Joyce-Liu vertex coproduct is instead defined by using the map
\oplus
:

(1)

We can define a direct sum pullback
\oplus^{*}:\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\to\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
using compatibility of the vanishing cycles functor with various sheaf operations.

(2)

We can define a translation, via pullback under the action
\textup{act}\colon\textup{B}\textbf{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}\to\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
, which scales the automorphisms,

(4)

\textup{act}^{*}\colon\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ \to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}\textbf{{G}}_{m})\ \simeq\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}[z]

(3)

We multiply resulting structure by the so called
Joyce-Borcherds
bicharacter

(5)

\Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\ =\ \sum_{k\geq 0}(-z)^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}-k}c_{k}(\textup{Ext})

of the two term complex of vector bundles
\textup{Ext}=\textup{RHom}(-,-)\in\textup{Perf}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}).

The result below understands this structure as being a (braided colocal)
vertex coproduct
, a definition due to Borcherds and Hubbard
[
4
,
27
]
; see section
1.6
. Loosely speaking, it is meant to capture the notion of a vector “sitting at” a point in
C
splitting into two vectors sitting at
z_{1},z_{2}\in\textbf{{C}}
, and this assignment depending meromorphically on
z=z_{1}-z_{2}
; see
[
2
]
.

Theorem A

(Theorem
2.3.1
)
.

Let
Q
be any quiver with potential
W
and a torus action that leaves the potential invariant, and assume it satisfies the
T
-equivariant Künneth property (
31
).
2
2

2

This is known to hold if
T=1
or in canonical tripled cases, see section
2.1.2
for more discussion.
. Then

\displaystyle\Delta(z)\ :\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\

\displaystyle\to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}((z^{-1}))

(6)

\displaystyle\alpha\

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \ \Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{1}\oplus^{*}(\alpha)

defines a coassociative (alias noncolocal or weakly coassociative) vertex coalgebra. Furthermore,
\Delta(z)
is cocommutative (alias colocal) in the sense that we have

(7)

\Delta(z)=\sigma\cdot S(z)\Delta(-z)\textup{act}^{*}

with
S(z)=(-1)^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}}\frac{\Psi(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee},z)}{\Psi(\textup{Ext},z)}
and
\sigma
the involution swapping the factors with Koszul sign rule.

If the torus is trivial and the quiver symmetric, this says that the vertex coproduct is colocal up to a sign
S(z)=(-1)^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}}
.

0.2.
Localised versus vertex coproducts

0.2.1.

We also compare with another way of dealing with the singularities in (
5
) using
localised coproducts
. Both this and the vertex coproduct are attempting to define the pull-push “
q_{*}p^{*}
” along the right diagram (
3
), but with
q
not being proper this is not a priori well-defined.

The method of Davison
[
9
]
is to formally define
q_{*}
with the inverse of
q^{*}
composed with multiplication by its Euler class of its shifted tangent complex, which is a quotient
e(Q_{0})/e(Q_{1})
of cohomology classes. The resulting coproduct

\Delta_{\textup{loc}}\ :\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ \to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}[S^{-1}]

is therefore only well-defined if we localise a set
S
of cohomology classes.

The connection between the localised coproduct and the Joyce-Liu coproduct is given by an observation that the class
e(Q_{0})/e(Q_{1})
is exactly the Euler class of the complex
e(\textup{Ext})
. Furthermore, we have that

(8)

\textup{act}^{*}_{1}e(\textup{Ext})\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)

where on the left we pull back by the
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
action on the first factor then expand as a Laurent series in
z^{-1}
. This allows us to bypass the need to invert, at the cost of working with series in
z
, and gives the following comparison result.

Theorem B

(Theorem
3.3.2
)
.

There is a map

(9)

\textup{act}^{*}_{1}:\mathcal{A}^{T}_{{Q},{W}}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{T}_{{Q},{W}}[S^{-1}]\ \to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{{Q},{W}}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{T}_{{Q},{W}}((z^{-1}))

intertwining the Joyce–Liu vertex and Davison localised coproducts:
\textup{act}^{*}_{1}\cdot\Delta_{\textup{loc}}=\Delta(z)
.

We expect this should be viewed as a comparison result between algebraic structures on configuration spaces of the cohomology rings of
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
.

0.3.
Compatibility and bosonisation

0.3.1.

The first main result of this paper is to show that our two structures are compatible. We state the theorem for the sign twisted (c.f. section
4.4.16
) versions of the multiplication although we get analogous theorems for the usual CoHA.

Theorem C

(Theorem
4.5.2
symmetric case, Theorem
4.5.5
general case)
.

The (
\psi
sign twisted) CoHA product
\star
and Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct
\Delta(z)
on together form a vertex bialgebra on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
; in particular,

\Delta(b\star b^{\prime},z)\ =\ \Delta(b,z)\star_{R(z)}\Delta(b^{\prime},z)

where on the right we have braided by the spectral
R
matrix

(10)

R(z)\ =\ \frac{\Psi(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee},z)}{\Psi(\textup{Ext},z)}

before multiplying the first/third and second/fourth factors.
3
3

3

Note that this
R
-matrix agrees with the operator
S(z)
in Theorem
A
up to a sign.

The form of Theorem
C
suggests that the CoHA
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
is a bialgebra object internal to some “meromorphic” braided monoidal category, with braiding given by the spectral
R
-matrix
R(z)
. Indeed, to state the bialgebra axioms one is forced to work within a category with at least a braided monoidal structure.

We will consider the category of modules over the
tautological subring

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\subseteq\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})
of the cohomology of the moduli stack, which is generated by chern classes of tautological bundles. Joyce’s
R
-matrix splits as

R_{d_{1},d_{2}}(z)\ =\ z^{\chi(d_{1},d_{2})-\chi(d_{2},d_{1})}R_{\textup{taut},d_{1},d_{2}}(z)

consisting of a rational function which is trivial in the symmetric case, and the component
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
is tautological-valued:

R_{\textup{taut}}(z)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}[[z^{-1}]].

Thus
B=\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
and
H=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
D
below.

In the non-symmetric case we do not give such an interpretation since in that case the category of tautological ring modules is
not
braided. See subsection
4.5.4
for some more discussion.

0.3.2.
Bosonisation

Theorem D

(Theorem
5.2.2
)
.

Let
B
be a vertex bialgebra inside
H\text{-}\textup{Mod}
, for
H
a holomorphic
4
4

4

See section
1.6.3
for a definition, and Lemma
1.6.4
.
vertex bialgebra with commutative multiplication and spectral quasitriangular element
R(z)
. Then the vector space

B\rtimes H\ =\ B\otimes H

has a canonical vertex bialgebra structure, with explicit formulas (
72
) and (
73
).

This should be viewed as a Tannakian reconstruction of the algebra whose category of modules is
B\text{-}\textup{Mod}(H\text{-}\textup{Mod})
. The vertex coproduct comed from structure on this category. In the case of ordinary bialgebras the procedure of constructing
B\rtimes H
from a bialgebra
B
in
H\text{-}\textup{Mod}
is known as bosonisation, discovered by Majid and Radford
[
41
,
44
]
.

Corollary E

(Theorem
5.0.1
)
.

Let
Q
be a symmetric quiver with potential. Then the extended CoHA

(11)

\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi,\textup{ext}}_{Q,W}\ =\ \mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}\rtimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}},

whose underlying vector space is
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}_{T}(\textup{pt})}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
, has a canonically defined vertex bialgebra structure, whose “extended CoHA” product (
66
) and “extended Joyce–Liu” vertex coproduct (
67
), e.g.

\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b\otimes h,z)\ =\ \left(\Delta(b,z)_{(1)}\otimes R^{(2)}(z)\Delta(h,z)_{(1)}\right)\otimes\left(R^{(1)}(z)\cup\Delta(b,z)_{(2)}\otimes\Delta(h,z)_{(2)}\right).

0.4.
Obtaining Drinfeld’s coproduct on ADE Yangians

0.4.1.

For a semisimple finite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, Drinfeld
[
16
]
defined a deformation of the universal enveloping loop algebra

\textup{gr}_{\hbar}Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\ \simeq\ \mathrm{U}(\mathfrak{g}[u])

called the
Yangian

Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
. This carries three algebraic structures: an associative product, an ordinary
standard
coproduct, and the
deformed Drinfeld coproduct

\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)\ :\ Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\ \to\ Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{\hbar}Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})((z^{-1}))

whose definition was finally worked out in full in
[
23
,
22
]
. We recall it in section
6.2.6
.

As a vector space the Yangian admits a triangular decomposition

Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\ =\ Y^{<0}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes Y_{\hbar}^{0}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes Y^{>0}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})

where each tensor factor is a subalgebra and
\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)
preserves the positive and negative Borel parts
Y^{\geq 0}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
,
Y^{\leq 0}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
: they are sub vertex bialgebras.

0.4.2.

By combining the work of Yang–Zhao
[
55
]
, Schiffmann-Vasserot
[
49
]
and an algebraic dimensional reduction theorem
[
46
,
56
]
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathbf{C}[\hbar]
-algebras

(12)

Y^{>0}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \mathcal{A}^{T,\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

where
\mathcal{A}^{T,\chi}_{\tilde{Q},\tilde{W}}
is the cohomological Hall algebra of the tripled Dynkin quiver
\widetilde{Q}
with canonical cubic potential, an an appropriately chosen torus.

In this setting, in Proposition
6.1.9
we compute the Joyce–Liu extended coproduct on spherical elements of tripled quivers. Their value is very simple: they are all vertex-primitive, and so under the isomorphism (
12
) corresponds to the “unbosonised Drinfeld” vertex coproduct

\Delta_{\textup{uDr}}(x_{i}^{+}(u),z)\ =\ x^{+}_{i}(u-z)\otimes 1+1\otimes x_{i}^{+}(u).

Then identifying
Y^{0}(\mathfrak{g})
with the tautological cohomology ring, we obtain the deformed Drinfeld coproduct as a bosonisation:

Theorem F

(Theorem
6.2.7
)
.

For
Q
an ADE quiver, there is an isomorphism

(13)

f\ :\ Y^{\geq 0}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \mathcal{A}^{T,\chi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

identifying the Drinfeld coproduct
\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)
and the Joyce–Liu coproduct
\Delta(z)
.

An analogous result was first obtained in
[
54
, Thm.B]
.

0.4.3.
Outlook: Maulik-Okounkov Yangians

Due to work of Cao, Maulik, Okounkov, Zhou and Zhou
[
43
,
7
]
one may define a Yangian
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W})
for any symmetric quiver with potential, which one expects to come with a standard coproduct as well as a Drinfeld coproduct

\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)\ :\ Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W})\ \to\ Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W})\otimes_{\hbar}Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W})((z^{-1})),

and with
R
-matrices intertwining the two coproducts and their opposites with each other.
5
5

5

Here
\hbar
denotes the equivariant parameters of the torus acting.
The analogue of (
12
) has been shown for tripled quivers with potential by Botta–Davison and Schiffmann–Vasserot
[
5
,
50
]
and is expected for general quivers with potential. The diagonal part of the
R
-matrix for
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,W})
is expected to match up with this paper’s
R(z)
.

We expect our method of explicitly computing bosonised vertex coproducts on spherical elements can be extended to symmetric quivers with potential, so long as we have
T
-equivariant Künneth assumption. Therefore, given any equivalence as in (
12
) one should be able to identify the Drinfeld coproduct with the Joyce vertex coalgebra when restricted to the spherically generated subalgebra. For instance, by
[
11
]
,
[
48
]
,
[
28
]
and
[
12
]
, this gives Drinfeld type coproducts for the affine Yangians.

0.5.
Why should such structure exist?

0.5.1.

We briefly explain two guiding points of view which motivated this work.

0.5.2.
Quantum groups

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra with triangular decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}^{-}\oplus\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{+}
. We consider the quantum group

U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})\ \in\ \textup{BiAlg}(\textup{Rep}_{q}T)

It is a bialgebra satisfying
\Delta(e_{i})=e_{i}\otimes 1+1\otimes e_{i}
for every generator
e_{i}\in\mathfrak{n}^{+}
; see
[
21
]
. This has the additional structure of a Nichols algebra
[
1
]
. Here,
\textup{Rep}_{q}T
is the category vector spaces graded over the weight lattice
\Lambda_{G}
, viewed as a braided monoidal category with braiding

\sigma q^{\kappa(\lambda,\mu)}\ :\ \textbf{{C}}_{\lambda}\otimes\textbf{{C}}_{\mu}\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \textbf{{C}}_{\mu}\otimes\textbf{{C}}_{\lambda}.

We view an object as a module over the group algebra
\textbf{{C}}[\Lambda_{G}]
, where a generator
k_{\nu}
acts as multiplication by
q^{\kappa(\nu,-)}
.

In particular,
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})
is
not
a subbialgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{b})\subseteq U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
; instead we construct
U_{q}(\mathfrak{b})
by
bosonisation
: it is the algebra whose category of modules is
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})\text{-}\textup{Mod}(\textbf{{C}}[\Lambda_{G}]\text{-}\textup{Mod})
, which for generic
q
recovers the usual definition

U_{q}(\mathfrak{b})\ \simeq\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})\rtimes\textbf{{C}}[\Lambda_{G}],

by
[
40
, 33.1.5]
or
[
1
, Thm.4.2]
. The coproduct is then
\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(e_{i})=e_{i}\otimes 1+k_{i}\otimes e_{i}
, which now
does
agree with the coproduct in
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. One can then take Drinfeld double of to obtain all of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.

0.5.3.
Hall algebras

By a result of Ringel and Green
[
51
, Thm.3.16]
, the Hall algebra of
\textbf{{F}}_{q}
-representations of an ADE quiver
Q
, is when extended by the Grothendieck group isomorphic to the Borel quantum group

\textbf{{H}}_{Q}\rtimes\textup{K}_{0}(\textup{Rep}_{\textbf{{F}}_{q}}Q)\ \simeq\ U_{q}(\mathfrak{b}).

The coproduct on
\textbf{{H}}_{Q}
is due to Green
[
51
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.4]
.

Comparing our paper to the above two stories, the analogy goes as follows:

finite quantum groups

U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{+})

\Delta

\textup{Rep}_{q}T

q^{\kappa}

\kappa

\Lambda_{G}

\cdots

Hall algebras

\textbf{{H}}_{Q}

\Delta_{\text{Green}}

\textup{K}_{0}(\textup{Rep}_{\textbf{{F}}_{q}}Q)\text{-}\textup{Mod}

q^{\chi}

\chi=\#\textup{Ext}_{\textbf{{F}}_{q}}

\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})

\cdots

CoHAs - this paper

\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}

\Delta(z)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\text{-}\textup{Mod}_{\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})\times\textbf{{Z}}}

R(z)

\chi=\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}

\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})

\cdots

0.5.4.
Physics

In physics, the category of modules over the CoHA
B=\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
is expected to be identified with the category of
line operators
of a certain
3d
holomorphic-topological quantum field theory
\partial\mathcal{T}
on
\textbf{{C}}\times\textbf{{R}}
, see
[
8
,
14
,
13
]
, and colliding these lines starting above points
z_{i},z_{j}\in\textbf{{C}}
is meant to correspond to the meromorphic tensor structure induced by the Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct
\Delta(z_{i}-z_{j})
.
B\text{-}\textup{Mod}
B\text{-}\textup{Mod}
B\text{-}\textup{Mod}

More precisely, it induces a
B
-module structure on
M\otimes N\left(\left((z_{i}-z_{j})^{-1}\right)\right)
for any pair of
B
-modules
M,N
. In particular, this gives a physics explanation for why we should expect a vertex coproduct on the CoHA (and nothing more).

This is expected to the boundary (or interface) of a
4d
holomorphic-topological quantum field theory on
\textbf{{C}}\times\textbf{{R}}^{2}
, obtained by twisting
[
18
]
the reduction of string theory along a Calabi–Yau threefold
X
, whose category of coherent sheaves are locally modelled on
(Q,W)
. This is the physics heuristic for the relation between the Calabi–Yau-three category and the (double) CoHA.

For instance, as this paper was being finished, Tudor Dimofte and Loïc Bramley informed us about ongoing work related to CoHAs and vertex coalgebras. In particular, they use a Tannakian reconstruction approach on the category of line operators of the IR limit of a
4d
holomorphic-topologically twisted QFT to produce Yangian-like algebras equipped with standard and vertex coproducts. In this case, the category of line operators is expected to be related to BPS states and the algebra produced is conjecturally the double of the CoHA. The corresponding vertex coproduct is expected to match the extended Joyce-Liu coproduct on the nonnegative half.

0.6.
Relation to the work of Dotsenko–Mozgovoy

0.6.1.

When we take a symmetric quiver
Q
and set the potential
W=0
then the CoHA
\mathcal{A}_{{Q},{0}}
becomes a supercommutative algebra. We can then dualise the algebra structure to obtain a cocommutative coalgebra and dualise the Joyce vertex coalgebra to obtain that
(\mathcal{A}_{{Q},{0}})^{\vee}
is a cocommutative coalgebra with a compatible vertex algebra structure. In this setting we can then apply a Milnor-Moore type theorem
[
26
]
to conclude that

Theorem G

(Theorem
7.2.2
)
.

The dual of the CoHA is a universal chiral envelope

(14)

(\mathcal{A}_{{Q},{0}})^{\vee}\simeq\textup{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{Q})

where
P_{Q}
is a vertex Lie algebra defined as the primitives of the dual of the CoHA multiplication.

In
[
15
]
, the authors give an explicit generators and relations definition of a vertex algebra on the vector space
(\mathcal{A}_{{Q},{0}})^{\vee}
and show it is compatible.

Theorem H

(Theorem
7.3.1
)
.

The Joyce vertex algebra structure on
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q,0}
and the vertex algebra in
[
15
]
are isomorphic.

Therefore, for a symmetric quiver with no potential we obtain a completely explicit generators and relations computation of the Joyce vertex algebra. Finally, as a corollary of Theorem
G
we get another proof of cohomological integrality originally proved in
[
17
]
.

1.
Preliminaries

In this section we introduce the moduli stacks of quiver representations.

1.1.
Quivers with potential

1.1.1.
Quivers

i
\textup{wt}(e)
e

A
quiver
is a directed graph
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
whose sets
Q_{0},Q_{1}
of vertices
i
and edges
e
are finite. It is
graded
with respect to a lattice
N\simeq\textbf{{Z}}^{r}
if we have a weight function
\textup{wt}\colon Q_{1}\to N
assigning an element of the lattice
\textup{wt}(e)\in N
to each edge. Write
s(e),t(e)
for the source and target of the edge.

The opposite quiver
Q^{\textup{op}}
is formed by reversing the direction of all arrows, and negating all gradings:
\textup{wt}^{\textup{op}}(e^{*})=-\textup{wt}(e)
where
e^{*}
is the reversed version of
e
. We call a quiver
symmetric
if
Q=Q^{\textup{op}}
and graded quiver
graded symmetric
if
(Q,\textup{wt})\simeq(Q^{\textup{op}},\textup{wt}^{\textup{op}})
.

1.1.2.
Representations

A
representation
of
Q
is an assignment of a finite dimensional vector space
V_{i}
to each vertex and a linear map
\rho_{e}
to each edge

\rho_{e}
V_{i}
V_{j}
\cdots

This is equivalent to a finite dimensional left module over the
path algebra

\textbf{{C}}Q
of
Q
, whose basis is the set of paths in
Q
and multiplication is given by concatenation of paths. The
dimension
vector of a representation is the element
d=(\dim V_{i})_{i\in Q_{0}}\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}
, writing
d_{i}
for the dimension at the
i
th vertex. We write
\delta_{i}
for the dimension vector which is
1
at vertex
i
and zero elsewhere.

The category
\textup{Rep}Q
of representations of
Q
is an abelian category, and the rank of the derived Hom spaces

\chi(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\ =\ \dim(\textup{Hom}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}))-\dim(\textup{Ext}^{1}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})),

is called the
Euler form
, which only depends on the dimension vectors
d,d^{\prime}
of
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, and is

\chi(d,d^{\prime})\ =\ \sum_{i}d_{i}d^{\prime}_{i}-\sum_{e:i\to j}d_{i}d^{\prime}_{j}.

1.1.3.
Potential

A
potential
of
Q
is an element
W\in\textbf{{C}}Q/[\textbf{{C}}Q,\textbf{{C}}Q]
.

A potential is given by a linear combination of cyclic words in
Q
, where two cyclic words are considered to be the same if one can be cyclically permuted to be the other. If
W
is a single cyclic word and
e\in Q_{1}
, then we define

\frac{\partial W}{\partial e}\ =\ \sum_{W=cec^{\prime}}c^{\prime}c

and we extend this definition linearly to general
W
. The
Jacobi algebra
associated to
(Q,W)
is defined to be

\textup{Jac}(Q,W)\ \vcentcolon=\ \textbf{{C}}Q/\left\langle\frac{\partial W}{\partial e}\,:\,e\in Q_{1}\right\rangle.

The direct sum and tensor product of representations are defined vertex-wise.

1.2.
Doubled and tripled quivers

1.2.1.
Preprojective and Jacobi algebras

The
double

\overline{Q}
and
triple

Q^{(3)}
of a quiver
Q
are quivers formed by adding a copy
e^{*}:j\to i
of each edge
e:i\to j
in the opposite direction, and then adding a loop
\omega_{i}
to each vertex, respectively.
i
e
Q
i
e
e^{*}
\overline{Q}
i
e
e^{*}
\omega_{i}
\widetilde{Q}

The
preprojective algebra
is the quotient by the two-sided ideal

\Pi_{Q}\ =\ \textbf{{C}}\overline{Q}/\left\langle\textstyle{\sum}_{e}[e,e^{*}]\right\rangle.

The
canonical tripled potential
on
\widetilde{Q}
is

\widetilde{W}\ =\ \left(\sum_{i}\omega_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{e}[e,e^{*}]\right).

We have an isomorphism

\Pi_{Q}[\omega]\ \simeq\ \textup{Jac}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W})

for the polynomial ring
\Pi_{Q}[\omega]
, sending
\omega\mapsto\sum\omega_{i}
.

1.3.
Moduli stacks of quiver representations

1.3.1.

The
moduli stack of representations
of
Q
of dimension
d
is the quotient stack

\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}\ \vcentcolon=\ \textup{Rep}_{d}(Q)/\textup{GL}_{d}

of the following vector space by the following group:

\displaystyle\textup{Rep}_{d}(Q)

\displaystyle\ \vcentcolon=\ \bigsqcap_{e:i\to j}\textup{Hom}(\textbf{{C}}^{d_{i}},\textbf{{C}}^{d_{j}})

\displaystyle\textup{GL}_{d}

\displaystyle\ \vcentcolon=\ \bigsqcap_{i\in Q_{0}}\textup{GL}_{d_{i}}

where the group
\textup{GL}_{d}
acts on
\textup{Rep}_{d}(Q)
by conjugation. The union over all dimension vectors is the moduli stack of objects of the abelian category
\textup{Rep}Q
in the sense of
[
53
]
. For instance, its
C
-points are precisely the groupoid of finite dimensional representations of
Q
.

1.3.2.
Torus equivariance

Let
Q
be a quiver graded by the lattice
N
of a torus
T=\textup{Hom}(N,\textup{{G}}_{m})
, with its grading denoted
\textup{wt}:Q_{1}\to N
as before. Thus we have an action of the torus on the representation space

T\times\textup{Rep}_{d}(Q)\ \to\ \textup{Rep}_{d}(Q),\hskip 42.67912ptt\cdot\rho_{e}\ =\ t^{\textup{wt}(e)}\rho_{e}

where
t^{\textup{wt}(e)}\in\textup{{G}}_{m}
is the image of an element of the torus under the character
\lambda_{e}:T\to\textup{{G}}_{m}
associated to
\textup{wt}(e)\in N
, which acts by multiplication on the element of the vector space
\rho_{e}\in\textup{Hom}(\textbf{{C}}^{d_{i}},\textbf{{C}}^{d_{j}})
.

The homomorphism
\lambda_{e}
gives a morphism of stacks
\lambda_{e}:\textup{B}T\to\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
. This defines a cohomology class

\mathbf{t}(e)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{2}(\textup{B}T,\textbf{{Q}})\ \simeq\ N

defined by the pullback along
\lambda_{e}
of the first chern class of the tautological bundle on
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
. Note that each weight
\textup{wt}(e)
also defines a line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{e}
on
\textup{B}T
. We will sometimes abuse notation and write

(15)

\textup{wt}(e)\ =\ c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{e})\ =\ \mathbf{t}(e)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{2}(\textup{B}T,\textbf{{Q}}).

1.3.3.
Tautological bundles and
T
-equivariant moduli space

We then define

\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}\ =\ \textup{Rep}_{d}(Q)/(\textup{GL}_{d}\times T).

The
tautological bundle

\mathcal{E}_{i,d}
for vertex
i
is the pullback of the universal vector bundle along

\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}\ \to\ \textup{B}\textup{GL}_{d}\times\textup{B}T\ \to\ \textup{B}\textup{GL}_{d_{i}}\times\textup{B}T\ \to\ \textup{B}\textup{GL}_{d_{i}}.

Its fibre over a point corresponding to a representation
\rho
is the vector space
V_{i}
. The
tautological map
of vector bundles
\rho_{e}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to\mathcal{E}_{j}
attached to any edge
e
is induced by the map

\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}\ \to\ \textup{Hom}(\textbf{{C}}^{d_{i}},\textbf{{C}}^{d_{j}})/\textup{GL}_{d_{i}}\times\textup{GL}_{d_{j}}.

Its fibre over a point is the linear map
\rho_{e}:V_{i}\to V_{j}
.

1.3.4.
Ext complex

The
Ext complex
is the two-term complex of vector bundles on
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{\prime}}
, given by

(16)

\textup{Ext}_{d,d^{\prime}}\ =\ \left(\bigoplus_{i}\mathcal{E}_{i,d}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\ \to\ \bigoplus_{e\ :\ i\to j}\mathcal{E}_{i,d}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{j,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\ \otimes\ \mathcal{L}_{e}\right).

We denote the zeroeth and first factors by
\textup{Ext}_{0}
and
\textup{Ext}_{1}
. The differential is given by viewing the left summands as the the vector bundle of maps from
\mathcal{E}_{i}\boxtimes 1
to
1\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and composing with the tautological maps
\rho_{e}
for each edge.

We will often have to dualise and also have to pull back the
Ext
-complex by the swap map
\sigma:\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\to\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}
; for this we note that
\mathcal{L}_{e}^{\vee}
corresponds to the character
-\textup{wt}(e)
, and
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{L}_{e}=\mathcal{L}_{e}
.

1.3.5.
Relation to the potential

The weight function induces a
N
-grading on
\textbf{{C}}Q
, sending a path to the sum of the weights of its edges. We say the potential is
invariant
for the
T
action if
W
has weight zero under this grading:
\textup{wt}(W)=0
.

1.4.
Cohomology of representation stacks

1.4.1.

The cohomology of the stack of quiver representations is a product over connected components

(17)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})\ =\ \bigsqcap_{d\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T}),

where each factor is generated by the chern classes of its tautological bundles:

\displaystyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T})

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}_{T}(\textup{BGL}_{d})

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)[c_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})\ :\ i\in Q_{0},\,1\leq r\leq d_{i}]

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ \textup{Sym}_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\left\{x_{i,\alpha}\ :\ i\in Q_{0},\,1\leq\alpha\leq d_{i}\right\}

where
x_{i,\alpha}
is a chern root of the tautological bundle
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

1.4.2.
Euler classes of tautological bundles

The Euler class of the tautological bundle
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is
e(\mathcal{E}_{i,d})=\bigsqcap_{n=1}^{d_{i}}x_{i,n}
. Moreover, we have formulas that agree with the euler class formulas in
[
5
, Section 4.1]

Proposition 1.4.3
.

The Euler classes
e(\textup{Ext}_{0})_{d,d^{\prime}}
and
e(\textup{Ext}_{1})_{d,d^{\prime}}
are equal to
6
6

6

Note that here we have
+\mathbf{t}(e)=+\textup{wt}(e)
in the definition of
e(Q_{1})
, as opposed to
-\mathbf{t}(e)
from
[
5
]
.

e_{d,d^{{}^{\prime}}}(Q_{1})\ \vcentcolon=\ \bigsqcap_{e\,:\,i\to j}\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d_{i},d_{j}^{\prime})}(1\otimes x_{j,m}-x_{i,n}\otimes 1+\textup{wt}(e))\ \in\ \ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{{}^{\prime}}})

and

e_{d,d^{{}^{\prime}}}(Q_{0})\ \vcentcolon=\ \bigsqcap_{i\in Q_{0}}\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d_{i},d_{i}^{\prime})}(1\otimes x_{i,m}-x_{i,n}\otimes 1)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{{}^{\prime}}})

respectively.

Proof.

Easily follows from additivity of chern roots under tensor products. For instance,

\displaystyle e\left(\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{i,d}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigsqcap_{i\in Q_{0}}e(\mathcal{E}_{i,d}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i,d^{{}^{\prime}}})\ =\ \bigsqcap_{i\in Q_{0}}\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d_{i},d_{i}^{\prime})}(1\otimes x_{i,m}-x_{i,n}\otimes 1)

where the last equality follows since the chern roots are negated upon dualising. Similarly

\displaystyle e\left(\bigoplus_{e\in i\to j\in Q_{1}}\mathcal{E}_{i,d}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{j,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\ \otimes\ \mathcal{L}_{e}\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigsqcap_{e\,:\,i\to j}e(\mathcal{E}_{i,d}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{j,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\ \otimes\ \mathcal{L}_{e})

\displaystyle\ =\ \bigsqcap_{e\,:\,i\to j}\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d_{i},d_{j}^{\prime})}(1\otimes x_{j,m}-x_{i,n}\otimes 1+\textup{wt}(e)

∎

1.4.4.
Underlying monoidal structure

We write
\textup{Vect}_{\Lambda}^{T}
for the category of vector spaces with a “cohomological”
Z
-grading, a grading by the cone

\Lambda\ =\ \pi_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})\ =\ \textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}},

and with an action of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
, with trivial
\Lambda
-grading and cohomologically graded in the usual way.

For any pair of such objects

V\ =\ \bigoplus_{(d_{1},n_{1})\in\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}}V_{(d_{1},n_{1})},\hskip 42.67912ptW\ =\ \bigoplus_{(d_{2},n_{2})\in\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}}W_{(d_{2},n_{2})}

we define their
twisted tensor product
by

(18)

V\otimes_{T}W\ =\ \bigoplus_{(d,n)\in\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}}\bigoplus_{(d,n)=(d_{1}+d_{2},n_{1}+n_{2})}V_{(d_{1},n_{1})}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}W_{(d_{2},n_{2})}[\chi_{Q}(d_{1},d_{2})-\chi_{Q}(d_{2},d_{1})]

where
\chi_{Q}
is the Euler form of the quiver and the shift is by the cohomological
Z
degree. It is neither symmetric nor braided monoidal in general, see section
4.4
. If the quiver
Q
is symmetric, then the cohomological shift is trivial and the monoidal structure is symmetric.

1.5.
Cohomological Hall algebra structure

1.5.1.

Consider the correspondence of
T
-equivariant stacks

(19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{SES}^{T}_{Q,d_{1},d_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Then the cohomological Hall algebra structure on

(20)

\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ =\ \bigoplus_{d\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d},\varphi_{W,d}\textbf{{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}}[\dim\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}])

is defined as in
[
5
,
32
,
45
]
by induction along the above diagram, using the formula

\displaystyle m=p_{*}q^{*}

induced by the functoriality of vanishing cycles in the Appendix
A.1
.
Here we suppress the Thom–Sebastiani and isomorphism from the notation. This induces a map
m:\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\to\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
.

1.5.2.
Remark

The CoHA
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
will be
\Lambda
graded by connected component, but the cohomology
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})
or tautological ring
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
, defined in section
4.1
, will be defined in trivial graded degree
0\in\Lambda
.

1.6.
Vertex coalgebras

1.6.1.

We define now define a version of coassociative coalgebra whose coproduct depends on a point
z
on the complex plane “meromorphically", due to Borcherds and Hubbard
[
27
, Def. 2.1]
.

A
nonlocal (alias coassociative) vertex coalgebra
is a vector space
V
together with a
translation
endomorphism,
covacuum
covector and a
cofield
map

(21)

T\ :\ V\ \to\ V,\hskip 42.67912pt\epsilon\ :\ V\ \to\ k,\hskip 42.67912pt\Delta(z)\ :\ V\ \to\ V\otimes V((z^{-1}))

satisfying:

•

Compatibility with the translation,

(22)

\Delta(z)\cdot T\ =\ \frac{d}{dz}\Delta(z)+(1\otimes T)\cdot\Delta(z).

•

Vertex coassociativity (also known as weak coassociativity),

(23)

(\Delta(z)\otimes\textup{id})\Delta(w)\ =\ (\textup{id}\otimes\Delta(w))\Delta(z+w)

•

Compatibility with the covacuum: we have
(\epsilon\otimes\textup{id})\cdot\Delta(z)=\textup{id}
and
(\textup{id}\otimes\epsilon)\cdot\Delta(z)=\textup{id}+\mathcal{O}(z)
is
V[z]
-valued.

Let
\Lambda
be a commutative monoid equipped with a bilinear form
\chi
and
R
be a
Z
-graded commutative ring with augmentation. A
\Lambda
-graded nonlocal (alias coassociative) vertex coalgebra
is a
\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}
-graded
R
-module

V\ =\ \bigoplus_{(\lambda,n)\in\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}}V_{(\lambda,n)}

with the structure of a nonlocal vertex algebra as above with
\otimes
replaced with

\otimes_{R}[\chi(\lambda,\mu)-\chi(\mu,\lambda)]

as in (
18
). We equip
z
with
\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}
-degree
|z|=(0,2)
so that the maps (
21
) are
R
-linear with degree
(0,-2),(0,0),(0,0)
respectively. We call the
Z
-degree
cohomological
.
We write

(24)

\Delta_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(z)\ :\ V_{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\ \to\ V_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes_{R}V_{\lambda_{2}}((z^{-1}))

for the associated map of
R
-modules on graded pieces, which has cohomological degree
\chi(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1})-\chi(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, and (
23
) is equivalent to

(25)

(\Delta_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(z)\otimes\textup{id})\Delta_{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}(w)\ =\ (\textup{id}\otimes\Delta_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}(w))\Delta_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}(z+w).

In our setting, we will work with
\Lambda=(\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}},+)
the dimension lattice of a quiver
Q
, where
+
is the sum operation on dimension vectors. Our commutative ring will be
R=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
with the twisted monoidal structure as in subsection
1.4.4
.

1.6.2.
Remark

The analogue of cocommutativity for vertex coalgebras is colocality (also called the Jacobi identity), see for instance part (
6
) of Theorem
4.5.2
.

1.6.3.
Holomorphicity

A coassociative vertex coalgebra is called
holomorphic
if the cofield map factors as

\Delta(z)\ :\ V\ \to\ V\otimes_{R}V[z]\ \subseteq\ V\otimes_{R}V((z^{-1})),

i.e. if it “has no poles”. Just as in
[
20
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.4]
, we have

Lemma 1.6.4
.

If
V
is a coalgebra with locally nilpotent coderivation
T
, i.e.

(26)

(T\otimes\textup{id})\cdot\Delta+(\textup{id}\otimes T)\cdot\Delta\ =\ \Delta\cdot T

and
T^{n}v=0
for
n\gg 0
, then
\Delta(z)\ =\ (e^{zT}\otimes\textup{id})\Delta
defines a holomorphic vertex coalgebra, and every holomorphic vertex coalgebra takes this form.

Proof.

We show that the assignment

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \Delta(z)\ =\ (e^{zT}\otimes\textup{id})\Delta

\displaystyle\Delta(0)

\displaystyle\ \mapsfrom\ \Delta(z)

sets up a bijection between such data and holomorphic vertex coalgebras. To do this, it is enough to show that for any holomorphic vertex coalgebra we have that

\Delta(z)\ =\ (e^{zT}\otimes\textup{id})\cdot\Delta(0)

which follows from (
22
), as their
z^{n}
coefficients agree, since

\left(\frac{d}{dz}\right)^{n}\Delta(z)|_{z=0}\ =\ \textup{ad}_{T}^{n}\Delta(0)\ =\ \left(\frac{d}{dz}\right)^{n}(e^{zT}\otimes\textup{id})\cdot\Delta(0)|_{z=0}

where
\textup{ad}_{T}(-)=(-)\cdot T-(\textup{id}\otimes T)\cdot(-)
, and for the second equality we used the coderivation property (
26
).
∎

2.
Joyce–Liu vertex coalgebra for quivers with potential

Let
Q
be an arbitrary quiver with an arbitrary potential
W
, which we allow to be graded by the character lattice
N
of a torus
T
. In this section, we will construct a nonlocal vertex coalgebra structure on the cohomological Hall algebra

(27)

\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}=\bigoplus_{d\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d},\varphi_{W,d}\textbf{{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}}[\dim\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}]),

under a relative Künneth assumption.

2.1.
Geometric structures on quiver moduli stacks

2.1.1.

The vertex coproduct structure will be induced from the following geometric structures on the moduli stack and a function
\textup{tr}W:\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\to\textbf{{A}}^{1}
coming from the potential:

(1)

The direct sum of quiver representations gives a map

\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}

which is commutative, associative, and the
0
representation
\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{0}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}
defines a unit.

(2)

The direct sum and potential are compatible in the sense that

(28)

\oplus\cdot\textup{tr}W\ =\ \textup{tr}W\boxplus\textup{tr}W,\hskip 42.67912pt0\cdot\textup{tr}W\ =\ 0.

(3)

There is an action

(29)

\textup{act}\ :\ \textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\ \to\ \mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}

of the group stack
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
compatible with the above structures: the above maps are
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
-equivariant, and
\textup{act}\cdot\textup{tr}W=0\boxplus\textup{tr}W
.

(4)

We have a perfect complex

(30)

\textup{Ext}\ \in\ \textup{Perf}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})

satisfying

\displaystyle(\oplus\times\textup{id})^{*}\textup{Ext}

\displaystyle\simeq\textup{Ext}_{13}\oplus\textup{Ext}_{23}

\displaystyle(\textup{id}\times\oplus)^{*}\textup{Ext}

\displaystyle\simeq\textup{Ext}_{12}\oplus\textup{Ext}_{13}

\displaystyle\textup{act}^{*}_{1}\textup{Ext}

\displaystyle\simeq\gamma^{-1}\boxtimes\textup{Ext}

\displaystyle\textup{act}^{*}_{2}\textup{Ext}

\displaystyle\simeq\gamma\boxtimes\textup{Ext}

where
\textup{Ext}_{ij}=\pi^{*}_{ij}\textup{Ext}
.

For trivial torus
T
in the quiver case, the direct sum map (1) can be viewed as arising from contravariance of the moduli of objects functor of
[
53
]
. Indeed,
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
is an open substack of the moduli of objects
\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)}
of the dg category
\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)
of quiver representations. The direct sum map on
\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)}
is induced by the diagonal map
\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)\to\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)\times\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)\simeq\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)\sqcup\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)
, and the direct sum on
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
is given by restriction.

For (2), the function
\textup{tr}W
is then given by restriction of a function on
\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)}
induced by a Hochschild homology class of the category
\textup{D}(\textup{Rep}Q)
. Then the compatibility with direct sum pullback in
(2)
follows again by restriction from the discussion in
[
31
, Prop 8.43]
.

Loosely speaking, (3) and (4) follow since every point of
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
contains a
\textbf{{G}}_{m}
in its stabiliser and by setting
Ext
to be given by
\textup{RHom}(-,-)
. More precisely, it is the pullback along
q
of the tangent complex of the map
p
in (
19
), with explicit description given in equation (
16
).

2.1.2.
Künneth isomorphism assumption

We need to assume

(31)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1]}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}},\varphi_{\textup{tr}W_{d_{1}}}\boxtimes\varphi_{\textup{tr}W_{d_{2}}})\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}},\varphi_{\textup{tr}W_{d_{1}}})\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}},\varphi_{\textup{tr}W_{d_{2}}}).

This is trivially true when
T=1
by the global Künneth isomorphism, but as discussed in appendix
A.1.1
the relative Künneth formula is in general not true.

Nevertheless, (
31
) is known to hold for tripled quivers with canonical tripled potential and appropriate torus action the assumption is also known by
[
10
, Thm. 9.6]
. It is also known in the case of
W=0
and more generally by the argument in
[
11
, Thm. 3.4]
, which holds whenever
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}
has a pure mixed Hodge structure.

2.2.
Structures on critical cohomology

We use the geometric structures on
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
to induce structures on vanishing cycles cohomology. The structures will be induced using the vanishing cycles toolkit in the Appendix
A.1
.

2.2.1.
Direct sum

The direct sum map on the moduli stack induces a map

\oplus^{*}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}).

The Künneth isomorphism does not apply here since
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
has infinitely many connected components, but restricting to the component labelled by a pair of dimension vectors
d_{1},d_{2}
it does, and we have a map

\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}})\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{2}}).

We now apply a similar argument to equivariant critical cohomology.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Assume we have a graded quiver with potential that satisfies Assumption
2.1.2
. There is a cocommutative coproduct with coderivation

\oplus^{*}\ :\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ \to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}[\Delta],\hskip 42.67912ptT\ :\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ \to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}[2],

where we shift by cohomological degree
\Delta=\chi(d_{1})+\chi(d_{2})-\chi(d_{1}+d_{2})
.

Proof.

To simplify notation, we write
\textbf{{Q}}_{d}
for
\textbf{{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T}}[\chi(d)]
. We have the following chain of maps

\displaystyle\varphi_{W_{d_{1}+d_{2}}}\textbf{{Q}}_{d_{1}+d_{2}}

\displaystyle\ \to\ \oplus_{*}\oplus^{*}\varphi_{W_{d_{1}+d_{2}}}\textbf{{Q}}_{d_{1}+d_{2}}

\displaystyle\ \to\ \oplus_{*}\varphi_{\oplus^{*}W_{d_{1}+d_{2}}}\textbf{{Q}}_{d_{1}+d_{2}}

\displaystyle\ =\ \oplus_{*}\varphi_{W_{d_{1}}\boxplus W_{d_{2}}}\textbf{{Q}}_{d_{1}+d_{2}}

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ \oplus^{*}(\varphi_{W_{d_{1}}}\textbf{{Q}}_{d_{1}}\boxtimes\varphi_{W_{d_{2}}}\textbf{{Q}}_{d_{2}}).

of sheaves on
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}}
. The first is the unit for the
(\oplus^{*},\oplus_{*})
adjunction, the second is the functoriality of vanishing cycles under pullback, the third is compatibility between
W
and the direct sum map, and the last is the Thom-Sebastiani isomorphism for vanishing cycles. Taking derived global sections gives

\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}}:\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}},\varphi_{W_{d_{1}+d_{2}}})\to\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}},\varphi_{W_{d_{1}}})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}},\varphi_{W_{d_{2}}})[\chi(d_{1})+\chi(d_{2})-\chi(d_{1}+d_{2})]

by the Künneth isomorphism assumption. Coassociativity and cocommutativity are inherited from associativity and commutativity of
\oplus
. For instance, it is coassociative because the diagram of stacks
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}+d_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\times\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{id}\times\oplus}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

commutes. Using the map
0:\textup{pt}\to\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
we construct the counit similarly.

We apply an identical argument to the action by
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
to get the following map of sheaves on
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}
, where the only difference is we use the compatibility between
W
and the action map
act
.

\displaystyle\varphi_{W_{d}}

\displaystyle\ \to\ \textup{act}_{*}\textup{act}^{*}\varphi_{W_{d}}\ \to\ \textup{act}_{*}\varphi_{\textup{act}^{*}W_{d}}\ =\ \textup{act}_{*}\varphi_{0\,\boxplus W_{d}}\ \simeq\ \textup{act}_{*}(\textbf{{Q}}_{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}}\boxtimes\varphi_{W_{d}}).

This induces a map

\textup{act}^{*}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d},\varphi_{W_{d}})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m})\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d},\varphi_{W,d})\ =\ \textbf{{C}}[z]\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d},\varphi_{W,d})

where
z
is the first chern class of the tautological line bundle on
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
. Since
act
is a group stack action, this defines an action for the coalgebra
\textbf{{C}}[z]
. Moreover,
\oplus^{*}
is linear for this coaction, as follows from the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{d_{2}}\times\textup{B}\textbf{{G}}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}}\times\textup{B}\textbf{{G}}_{m}\times\textup{B}\textbf{{G}}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{d_{1}+d_{2}}\times\textup{B}\textbf{{G}}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}^{T}_{d_{1}+d_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{id}\times\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\times\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{act}\times\textup{act}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

Thus
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}
defines a coalgebra inside
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m})\text{-}\textup{CoMod}
. We thus define the endomorphism
T
to be the coefficient of
z
in the action map
\textup{act}^{*}=\exp(Tz)
, which is a coderivation as
z
is primitive.
∎

2.2.3.
W=0
case

In particular, by taking
W=0
we get a
\textbf{{C}}[z]
-coaction and coproduct on ordinary cohomology

\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q}\ =\ \bigoplus_{d\in\Lambda}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})[\dim\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}]

which we also denote by
\textup{act}^{*}
and
\oplus^{*}
. Note that
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q}
acts on
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
by cup product, and this algebra and module structure is compatible with the coproduct and
\textbf{{C}}[z]
-coaction as follows.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Denote the coproducts and translations on
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,0}
and
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
using the subscript
0
and
W
respectively. They are compatible as follows

\displaystyle\textup{act}^{*}_{W}(h\cdot b)\

\displaystyle=\ \textup{act}^{*}(h)_{0}\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{W}(b)

\displaystyle\oplus^{*}_{W}(h\cdot b)\

\displaystyle=\ \oplus^{*}_{0}(h)\cdot\oplus^{*}_{W}(b)

for each cohomology class
h\in\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,0}
and critical cohomology class
b\in\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
.

Proof.

The compatibility of direct sum with cup product follows from the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\times\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\times\oplus}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

and compatibility of act maps from

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})^{\times_{\textup{B}T}2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})^{\times_{\textup{B}T}2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\times\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{act}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{act}_{1}\times\textup{act}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

∎

We usually omit the subscripts
0
and
W
on coproducts and translations.

2.2.5.
Joyce–Borcherds twist

We next introduce the
Joyce–Borcherds twist

(32)

\Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z)\ =\ \sum_{k\geq 0}z^{\textup{rk}\theta-k}c_{k}(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}})\in\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}})\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}(\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}})((z^{-1})),

and sometimes write
\Psi(\textup{Ext},z)
to emphasise the role of the perfect complex
Ext
.
The following algebraic properties will follow from compatibilities of the complex
Ext
with respect to the different operations on our moduli stack
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
. We will also use properties of chern series of perfect complexes as in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 2.2.6
.

The Joyce–Borcherds twist satisfies

(\oplus_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{*}\times\textup{id})\Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}},z)\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{3}},z)\Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{2},d_{3}},z)

(\textup{id}\times\oplus_{d_{2},d_{3}}^{*})\Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}},z)\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z)\Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{3}},z)

and

\textup{act}_{1,w}^{*}\Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z)\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z-w),\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{act}_{2,w}^{*}\Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z)\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z+w).

Proof.

These properties all follow from the identities

(\oplus_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{*}\times\textup{id})\textup{Ext}_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}\ =\ \textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{3}}\oplus\textup{Ext}_{d_{2},d_{3}}

(\textup{id}\times\oplus_{d_{2},d_{3}}^{*})\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}\ =\ \textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\oplus\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{3}}

and

\textup{act}_{1}^{*}\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\ =\ \gamma^{-1}\boxtimes\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{act}_{2}^{*}\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\ =\ \gamma\boxtimes\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},

where we apply Lemma
A.2.8
on how
\Psi(-,z)
interacts with direct sums and tensoring with line bundles.
∎

2.3.
Main construction

Theorem 2.3.1
.

Let
Q
be an
N
-graded quiver with invariant potential
W
, which satisfies the Künneth assumption (
31
). Then

\displaystyle\Delta(z)\ :\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{{Q},{W}}\

\displaystyle\to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{{Q},{W}}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{{Q},{W}}((z^{-1}))

\displaystyle\alpha\

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{1}\oplus^{*}(\alpha)

defines a coassociative vertex coproduct linear over
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
.

In fact
\Delta(z)
is also braided colocal–the vertex analogue of braided cocommutativity–see the last part of Theorem
4.5.2
. Note that we twist by the
Ext
complex of the ambient smooth stack
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
, not of the Calabi–Yau-three moduli stack
\mathcal{M}_{Q,W}=\textup{Crit}(\textup{Tr}W)
inside it, as one might first have guessed.

Proof.

The counit will be induced by vanishing cycle pullback along the inclusion
\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{0}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
. The compatibility with translation will follow from
2.2.6
,
2.2.3
and that
\textup{act}^{*}_{z}=e^{zT}
, so
\frac{d}{dz}\textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}=(T\otimes 1)\textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}
.

In this proof we will freely use the compatibilities in Lemma
2.2.4
. We now give a direct proof of vertex coassociativity

(33)

(\Delta_{d_{1},d_{2}}(z)\otimes\textup{id})\Delta_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}(w)=(\textup{id}\otimes\Delta_{d_{2},d_{3}}(w))\Delta_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}(z+w)

following Liu
[
37
, 3.2.13]
, and using the shorthand
\Psi(z)=\Psi(\textup{Ext},z)
. Let us expand both sides starting with the left

\displaystyle(\Psi(-z)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\otimes\textup{id})\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}(\oplus_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{*}\otimes\textup{id})\ \left(\Psi(-w)_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}\textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ =\ (\Psi(-z)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\otimes\textup{id})\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\Psi(-w)_{d_{1},d_{3}}\Psi(-w)_{d_{2},d_{3}}(\oplus_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{*}\otimes\textup{id})\left(\textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ =\ \Psi(-z)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\Psi(-z-w)_{d_{1},d_{3}}\Psi(-w)_{d_{2},d_{3}}\ \textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}(\oplus_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{*}\otimes\textup{id})\textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}

where we have used the first and second parts of Proposition
2.2.6
in the first and second equality. Similarly, we can expand the right hand side to

\displaystyle(\textup{id}\otimes\Psi(-w)_{d_{2},d_{3}})\textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}(\textup{id}\otimes\oplus_{d_{2},d_{3}}^{*})\ \left(\Psi(-z-w)_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}\textup{act}_{z+w,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ =\ (\textup{id}\otimes\Psi(-w)_{d_{2},d_{3}})\textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}\Psi(-z-w)_{d_{1},d_{3}}\Psi(-z-w)_{d_{1},d_{2}}(\textup{id}\otimes\oplus_{d_{2},d_{3}}^{*})\left(\textup{act}_{z+w,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ =\ \Psi(-w)_{d_{2},d_{3}}\Psi(-z-w)_{d_{1},d_{3}}\Psi(-z)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\ \textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}(\textup{id}\otimes\oplus_{d_{2},d_{3}}^{*})\textup{act}_{z+w,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}.

The
\Psi
factors on left and right are the same, so it remains to show that

\textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}(\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\otimes\textup{id})\textup{act}^{*}_{w,1}\ =\ \textup{act}^{*}_{z+w,1}\textup{act}^{*}_{w,2}(\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\otimes\textup{id}).

But this is an easy consequence of the commutativity of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}\times\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\times\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{id}\times\oplus}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{act}\times\textup{act}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

which implies the first equality in

\textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}(\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\otimes\textup{id})\textup{act}^{*}_{w,1}\ =\ \textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}\textup{act}^{*}_{w,1}\textup{act}^{*}_{w,2}(\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\otimes\textup{id})\ =\ \textup{act}^{*}_{z+w,1}\textup{act}^{*}_{w,2}(\oplus^{*}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\otimes\textup{id}),

with the second following because
\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\textup{act}^{*}_{w}=\textup{act}^{*}_{z+w}
. This finishes the proof that
\Delta(z)
is a coassociative vertex coalgebra.
∎

3.
Localised coproducts

In section
2
we defined a graded vertex coproduct on
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}
, whose main piece of data is a map of vector spaces

\Delta(z)\ :\ \mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}\ \to\ \mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}((z^{-1})).

In this section we show that this data may be repacked in a more or less equivalent form, as a
localised
coproduct

\Delta_{\textup{loc}}\ :\ \mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}\ \to\ \mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T}\left[S^{-1}\right]

first defined by Davison
[
9
]
but frequently appearing in the CoHA literature
[
54
]
. To begin with give the definition of localised coproducts and relate them to vertex coproducts. In Theorem
3.3.2
we show that applying this construction to Davisons’
\Delta_{\textup{loc}}
recovers the Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct
\Delta(z)
.

We now give a definition of localised coproducts adapted from
[
9
]
. If the reader does not want to understand the abstract definition, they can jump to section
3.2
, where we recall the construction in
[
9
]
, which is the only localised coproduct we will use.

3.1.
Localised coproducts to vertex coproducts

3.1.1.

We work over a base
\mathbf{Z}
-graded commutative ring
R
. All tensor products are implicitly over
R
. Let
\mathcal{O}=(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
be a collection of graded commutative rings with a cocommutative graded coproduct

\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\ :\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha+\beta}\ \to\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}

and
S_{\alpha,\beta}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}
a collection of multiplicative subsets satisfying

(34)

(\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\otimes\textup{id})S_{\alpha+\beta,\gamma}\ \sim\ S_{\alpha,\gamma,13}\cdot S_{\beta,\gamma,23},\hskip 42.67912pt(\textup{id}\otimes\delta_{\beta,\gamma})S_{\alpha,\beta+\gamma}\ \sim\ S_{\alpha,\beta,12}\cdot S_{\alpha,\gamma,13}

where equivalence means that the associated localisations of
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
are equal.

A
localised coalgebra
is a direct sum
A=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Lambda}A_{\alpha}
of
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
-modules with

\Delta_{\alpha,\beta}\ :\ A_{\alpha+\beta}\ \to\ A_{\alpha}\otimes A_{\beta}[S_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}]

maps of
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
-modules, i.e.
\Delta_{\alpha,\beta}(ba)=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}(b)\Delta_{\alpha,\beta}(a)
, which is coassociative,

(35)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\alpha}\otimes A_{\beta+\gamma}[S_{\alpha,\beta+\gamma}^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\alpha}\otimes(A_{\beta}\otimes A_{\gamma})[S_{\beta,\gamma,23}^{-1},(\textup{id}\otimes\delta_{\beta,\gamma})S_{\alpha,\beta+\gamma}^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\alpha+\beta+\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A_{\alpha}\otimes A_{\beta})\otimes A_{\gamma}[S_{\alpha,\beta,12}^{-1},(\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\otimes\textup{id})S_{\alpha+\beta,\gamma}^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\alpha+\beta}\otimes A_{\gamma}[S_{\alpha+\beta,\gamma}^{-1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}

and which satisfies the counit axioms with respect to a map
\epsilon:A_{0}\to k
. The vertical identification in (
35
) follows from (
34
).

3.1.2.
Remark

We will abuse notation and refer to

(36)

\Delta\ :\ A\ \to\ A\otimes A[S^{-1}]\ \vcentcolon=\ \bigoplus_{\alpha,\beta}A_{\alpha}\otimes A_{\beta}[S_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}]

as the localised coproduct.

3.1.3.

Now assume that all the above structures are equivariant for an action of
\textup{{G}}_{a}
. That is, we have coactions of
\textbf{{C}}[z]

\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\ =\ \exp(zT_{\mathcal{O}})\ :\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\ \to\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}[z],\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{act}_{A}\ =\ \exp(zT_{A})\ :\ A_{\alpha}\ \to\ A_{\alpha}[z]

given by algebra and module maps:

(37)

\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}(bb^{\prime})\ =\ \textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}(b)\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}(b^{\prime}),\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{act}_{A}(ba)\ =\ \textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}(b)\textup{act}_{A}(a),

such that
S_{\alpha,\beta}
is translation invariant:
7
7

7

To be precise, this means that the localisation of
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}[z]
by the multiplicative subsets
(\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}})S_{\alpha,\beta}
and
S_{\alpha,\beta}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}[z]
are isomorphic.

(38)

(\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}})S_{\alpha,\beta}\ \sim\ S_{\alpha,\beta},

and satisfying

(39)

\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\ =\ (\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}})\cdot\delta_{\alpha,\beta},\hskip 42.67912pt\Delta_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\textup{act}_{A}\ =\ (\textup{act}_{A}\otimes\textup{act}_{A})\cdot\Delta_{\alpha,\beta}

where the last equation is valued in the same localisation by translation invariance of
S
.

We call the above structure a
translation-equivariant localised coalgebra
.

3.1.4.
Producing vertex coproducts

Consider a translation equivariant localised coalgebra such that each

(\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes\textup{id})S_{\alpha,\beta}\ \in\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}((z^{-1}))

is an
invertible
element.

Definition 3.1.5
.

The maps

\delta_{\mathcal{O}}(z)\ :\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha+\beta}\ \to\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}((z^{-1})),\hskip 42.67912pt\Delta_{A}(z)\ :\ A_{\alpha+\beta}\ \to\ A_{\alpha}\otimes A_{\beta}((z^{-1}))

are defined by
\delta_{\mathcal{O}}(z)=\delta_{\mathcal{O}}\cdot\textup{act}
and
\Delta_{A}(z)=\Delta_{A}\cdot\textup{act}
using the following map:

\displaystyle\textup{act}\ :\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}[S_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}]

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}[z]\left[(\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes\textup{id})S_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ \hookrightarrow\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}((z^{-1}))\left[(\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes\textup{id})S_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ \simeq\ \mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\beta}((z^{-1}))

and likewise

\textup{act}\ :\ A_{\alpha}\otimes A_{\beta}[S_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}]\ \to\ A_{\alpha}\otimes A_{\beta}((z^{-1}))

sending
\frac{a\otimes a^{\prime}}{s}\ \mapsto\ \frac{\textup{act}_{A}(a)\otimes a^{\prime}}{\textup{act}(s)}
.

3.2.
Davisons’ localised coproduct on the CoHA

3.2.1.

We now consider
R=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
and
\mathcal{O}_{d}=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})
with its cup product, coproduct
\delta_{d,e}=\oplus^{*}_{d,e}
and multiplicative subset

S_{d_{1},d_{2}}\ =\ \langle e(\textup{Ext}_{0}),e(\textup{Ext}_{1})\rangle\ =\ \left\langle e(Q_{0}),e(Q_{1})\right\rangle

generated by the Euler class of the components of the
Ext
complex (
16
). We define

\textup{act}_{\mathcal{O}}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})[z]

as in Lemma
4.3.2
, acting on chern roots as
x_{i,n}\mapsto x_{i,n}+z
. This satisfies the above axioms for
\mathcal{O}
, working inside the linear monoidal category
\textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda}
with its product
\otimes_{T}
defined in (
18
).

3.2.2.
Remark

We could have equivalently used

S\ \sim\ \langle e(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i}),\,e(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{j}\,\otimes\,\mathcal{L}_{e})\ :\ e:i\to j\rangle.

3.2.3.

We now define a localised coproduct on
A=\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
, assuming the Künneth assumption (
31
). The
Davison localised coproduct
from
[
9
]
and
[
5
, Section 5.2]
is

\displaystyle\Delta_{\textup{loc}}\ :\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}

\displaystyle\ \to\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\left[S^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \frac{e(Q_{0})}{e(Q_{1})}\cdot(q^{*})^{-1}p^{*}\alpha

defined in terms of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{SES}^{T}_{Q,d_{1},d_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

and the equivariant Euler classes
e(Q_{0}),e(Q_{1})
as defined in Proposition
1.4.3
.

That is, we take use the pullbacks
p^{*},q^{*}
on critical cohomology as in (
114
), the latter being invertible because
q
is an affine fibration
8
8

8

Note it is important to consider the map
q\colon\textup{SES}^{T}_{Q,d_{1},d_{2}}\to\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}}
and not
\textup{SES}^{T}_{Q}\to\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}}
, since the latter is
not
an affine fibration.
, and the Thom–Sebastiani and Künneth isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}},\varphi_{W})

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{SES}^{T}_{Q,d_{1},d_{2}},\varphi_{W_{\textup{SES}}})

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\leftarrow}\limits}}\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}}\times_{BT}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}},\varphi_{W\boxplus W})
