\displaystyle\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}},\varphi_{W})\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}},\varphi_{W}).

In the middle we denoted the common function
p^{*}W=q^{*}(W\boxplus W)
by
W_{\textup{SES}}
.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

We have that

\Delta_{\textup{loc}}(\alpha)\ =\ e(\textup{Ext})\cdot\oplus^{*}(\alpha)

where
e(\textup{Ext})
is the localised Euler class (as defined in
A.2.6
) of the two-term complex
Ext
.

Proof.

We first note that
(q^{*})^{-1}=s^{*}
because
s
is a right inverse of
q
, so by functoriality of the pullback map we have

(40)

\oplus^{*}\ =\ (ps)^{*}\ =\ s^{*}p^{*}\ =\ (q^{*})^{-1}p^{*}.

We are then finished if we have

(41)

e(\textup{Ext})\ =\ \frac{e(Q_{0})}{e(Q_{1})},

which follows from Proposition
1.4.3
.
∎

Finally we let
\textup{act}_{A}
be the composition

\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m})\otimes\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ \simeq\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}[z],

using the construction in Lemma
2.2.2
, then

Proposition 3.2.5
.

\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
is a translation equivariant localised coproduct satisfying the properties in subsection
3.1.4
.

Proof.

The localised coproduct and
\textbf{{C}}[z]
-coaction are linear over
\mathcal{O}_{d}=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})
by Lemma
2.2.4
, proving (
37
) and (
39
). It is coassociative by
[
9
]
.

It remains to check that
S
satisfies the relevant axioms. It satisfies the first hexagon relation (
34
) for multiplicative subsets because

\displaystyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}}^{T})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}}^{T})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}}^{T})\left[(\oplus\times\textup{id})^{*}e(\textup{Ext}_{i})^{-1}\ :\ i=0,1\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}}^{T})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}}^{T})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}}^{T})\left[e(\textup{Ext}_{i})_{13}^{-1},e(\textup{Ext}_{i})_{23}^{-1}\ :\ i=0,1\right]

since
(\oplus\times\textup{id})^{*}e(\textup{Ext}_{i})=e(\textup{Ext}_{i})_{13}e(\textup{Ext}_{i})_{23}
, and likewise we have the second hexagon equation (
34
). Every element of
S
satisfies translation invariance (
38
) because

(\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\otimes\textup{act}_{z}^{*})e(\textup{Ext}_{i})\ =\ e\left((\textup{act}\times\textup{act})^{*}\textup{Ext}_{i}\right)\ =\ e(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{-1}\boxtimes\textup{Ext}_{i})\ =\ e(\textup{Ext}_{i})

as the
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
weights
(-1,1)
of the Ext complex
\textup{Ext}=(\textup{Ext}_{0}\to\textup{Ext}_{1})
sum to zero. Likewise, since the leading term of

(\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\otimes\textup{id})e(\textup{Ext}_{i})\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext}_{i},-z)\ =\ \pm z^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}_{i}}\mp c_{1}(\textup{Ext}_{i})z^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}_{i}-1}\pm\cdots

has nonzero leading term, it follows that it is invertible as a Laurent series in
z^{-1}
.
∎

Note the relation between the invertibility of elements in
(\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\otimes\textup{id})S
and
[
9
, Prop. 4.1]
.

3.3.
Recovering the Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct

3.3.1.

The main result in this section is that Davisons’ localised coproduct agrees with the Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct, up to the map
act
in Definition
3.1.5
interpolating between localised and vertex coproducts.

For the sake of explicitness, we note that in our case
act
takes a localised class, whose denominator is a product of
x_{i,n}\otimes 1-1\otimes x_{j,m}+\textup{wt}(e)
over edges
e:i\to j
, replaces any chern root in the first tensor factor of denominator and numerator with itself plus
z
, then Laurent expands in
z^{-1}
.

Theorem 3.3.2
.

The diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\left[S^{-1}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}((z^{-1}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\textup{loc}}}

commutes.

Proof.

The above diagrams factors as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}[S^{-1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}((z^{-1}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e(Q_{0})\textup{/}e(Q_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q^{*})^{-1}p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(\textup{Ext}\textup{,}-z)\cdot\textup{act}_{z\textup{,}1}}

whose left cell commutes by (
40
). It thus suffices to show that

\textup{act}\left(\frac{e(Q_{1})}{e(Q_{0})}\cdot\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}\right)\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}(\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}).

Using equation (
41
) we have that

\textup{act}\left(\frac{e(Q_{1})}{e(Q_{0})}\cdot\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}\right)\ =\ \textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\left(\frac{e(Q_{1})}{e(Q_{0})}\right)\cdot\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}\ =\ \textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}(e(\textup{Ext}))\cdot\alpha_{1}\otimes\alpha_{2}

so we are finished as soon as we know that

(42)

\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}(e(\textup{Ext}))\ =e(\textup{act}_{1}^{*}\textup{Ext})\ =\ e(\gamma^{-1}\boxtimes\textup{Ext})\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext},-z),

but the middle equality follows as
Ext
has
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
weight
-1
in the first factor, and the final equality follows from Proposition
A.2.10
. This proves the Theorem.
∎

Corollary 3.3.3
.

Consider
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W,d}

We have the following explicit formula for the Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct:

\Delta(\alpha,z)\ =\ \sum_{d^{\prime},d^{\prime\prime}}\frac{\bigsqcap_{i}\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d^{\prime}_{i},d^{\prime\prime}_{i})}(-z-x_{i,n}\otimes 1+x_{j,m}\otimes 1)}{\bigsqcap_{e\,:\,i\to j}\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d^{\prime}_{i},d^{\prime\prime}_{j})}(-z-x_{i,n}\otimes 1+x_{j,m}\otimes 1+\textup{wt}(e))}\ \cdot\ \textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus_{d^{\prime},d^{\prime\prime}}^{*}(\alpha)

where we sum over all dimension vectors
d,d^{{}^{\prime}}
with
d^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}=d
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that

\textup{act}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{\prime}})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{\prime\prime}})[S^{-1}]\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{\prime}})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{\prime\prime}})((z^{-1}))

acts on chern roots as
x_{i,n}\mapsto x_{i,n}+z
.
∎

4.
The critical CoHA is a bialgebra

In this section we will prove that the CoHA algebra structure is compatible with the Joyce-Liu vertex coalgebra. This culminates in Theorem
4.5.2
, where we also give a comprehensive list of algebraic structures and compatibilities on the CoHA. To formulate these compatibilities we now start by defining a particular subalgebra of the singular cohomology of
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
.

4.1.
The tautological ring

4.1.1.

In the following two sections we define a
tautological
subring of the cohomology

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\ \subseteq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})\ =\ \bigsqcap_{d\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})

as well as a
tautological part of the
R
-matrix

R_{\textup{taut}}(z)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}[[z^{-1}]],

and show that it is a commutative, cocommutative holomorphic vertex bialgebra.

4.1.2.

The
tautological ring
is the
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
-subalgebra of the cohomology of
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
generated by the chern characters of the tautological bundles:

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\ =\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)\langle\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})\ :\ r\geq 0,i\in Q_{0}\rangle\ \subseteq\ \bigsqcap_{d\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})\ =\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T}).

Equivalently, we can take the ring generated by the chern classes
c_{k}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

The tautological ring is a free polynomial
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
-algebra over the chern generators
\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
:

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)[\gamma_{i,r}\ :\ r\geq 0,i\in Q_{0}]\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})\ \mapsto\ \gamma_{i,r}.

Proof.

It is enough to show that the classes
\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
are algebraically independent over
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
. Since we have
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})\simeq\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})
, it is enough to show that the classes
\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
are algebraically independent in
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})
. Note that for any
d\in\textbf{{Z}}^{r}
, one can show that

\textbf{{C}}[x_{k}\ :\ 0<k\leq d]^{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\ \simeq\ \textbf{{C}}[p_{k}\ :\ 0<k\leq d]

is a polynomial algebra in the symmetric power sum
p_{k}=\sum_{i}x_{i}^{k}=\sum x_{i_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{r}}^{k_{r}}
. Thus any nontrivial polynomial in
\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
which vanishes in the tautological ring would, by taking its image in
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}_{T}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d})\simeq\textbf{{C}}[x_{k}\ :\ 0<k\leq d]^{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}
for any
d
greater than the
r
appearing in this polynomial, give a nontrivial relation betwen the
p_{k}
, which is impossible since symmetric power sums are algebraically independent.
∎

4.1.4.
Warning

Euler classes of the tautological bundles
\mathcal{E}_{i}
however usually do not lie in the tautological ring. Indeed, we have that

e(\mathcal{E}_{i})\ =\ \left(c_{\textup{rk}\mathcal{E}_{i,d}}(\mathcal{E}_{i,d})\right)\ \in\ \bigsqcap_{d\in\Lambda}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T}),

whose componentwise cohomological degree
\chi(d,\delta_{i})=2\textup{rk}\mathcal{E}_{i,d}
is generally not bounded above. This is in contrast to every element of the tautological ring, which is a finite sum of elements with finite cohomological degree.

For instance, if
Q=\bullet
and
\mathcal{E}
is the tautological vector bundle over
\mathcal{M}_{Q}=\textup{BGL}
then

e(\mathcal{E})\ =\ \left(1,\,x_{1},\,x_{1}x_{2},\,x_{1}x_{2}x_{3},\,\ldots\ \right)

is certainly not a polynomial in the symmetric power sums
p_{k}
.

4.2.
Tautological part of the
R
-matrix

4.2.1.

The
tautological part of the
R
-matrix
is

R_{\textup{taut}}(z)\ =\ \frac{c(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee},z^{-1})}{c(\textup{Ext},z^{-1})},

i.e.
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)=\left(R_{\textup{taut}}(z)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\right)_{d_{1},d_{2}\in\Lambda}
where

R_{\textup{taut}}(z)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\ =\ \frac{c(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}_{d_{2},d_{1}}^{\vee},z^{-1})}{c(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z^{-1})}\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{1}})\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{2}})[[z^{-1}]],

expanded as a geometric series in
z^{-1}
.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

This defines an element of

R_{\textup{taut}}(z)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}[[z^{-1}]].

Proof.

We expand the denominator as a geometric series, which gives
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
as a cohomology-valued power series in
z^{-1}
with coefficients polynomials in the nonzero chern classes of
Ext
and
\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee}
, hence the nonzero chern characters. By the explicit form (
16
) for the Ext complex and additivity of chern characters under direct sum, these are polynomials in the
z^{>0}
coefficients of

\textup{ch}(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\vee}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{j}\,\otimes\,\mathcal{L},z)\ =\ \textup{ch}(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\vee},z)\otimes\textup{ch}(\mathcal{E}_{j},z)\,\cdot\,\textup{ch}(\mathcal{L},z)

which are themselves
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
-polynomials in
\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{k})
for
r\geq 0
, hence lie in the tautological ring.
∎

In the ADE case this agrees with the ordinary tautological ring by Proposition
6.1.7
, and for general quivers with potential is probably the better definition to give.

4.2.3.

We will also consider the
full Joyce
R
-matrix

(43)

R(z)\ =\ \frac{\Psi(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee},z)}{\Psi(\textup{Ext},z)}\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})((z^{-1}))

which is non-tautological; see Proposition
4.4.15
for an explicit formula in terms of chern roots. We can see that

R(z)_{d_{1},d_{2}}\ =\ \frac{z^{\chi(d_{2},d_{1})}}{z^{\chi(d_{1},d_{2})}}\frac{c(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee}_{d_{2},d_{1}},z^{-1})}{c(\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}},z^{-1})}\ =\ \frac{z^{\chi(d_{2},d_{1})}}{z^{\chi(d_{1},d_{2})}}R_{\textup{taut}}(z)

and so if the quiver is symmetric then
R(z)
equals
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
.

4.2.4.
Classical limits

If the Ext complex is symmetric
[\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}]\simeq[\textup{Ext}]
as an elements in the Grothendieck group of
\mathcal{M}_{Q}\times\mathcal{M}_{Q}
, not necessarily
T
-equivariant, then the leading term of the
R
-matrix is the identity. This is the case where the quiver is symmetric, for instance in the tripled quiver case:

Proposition 4.2.5
.

If the quiver
Q
is symmetric (e.g. a tripled quiver) then we have that

R_{\textup{taut}}(z)\ =\ 1\otimes 1+\mathcal{O}(\hbar_{1},\ldots,\hbar_{r})

where
\mathcal{O}(\hbar_{1},\ldots,\hbar_{r})
is a power series whose coefficients as
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)=\textbf{{C}}[\hbar_{1},\ldots,\hbar_{r}]
-polynomials have zero constant term.

4.3.
Structures on the tautological ring

4.3.1.

We show that the geometric structures on the moduli stacks (section
2.1
) induce algebraic structures on the tautological ring. This more or less follows from section
2.2
applied to the case of zero potential function, the only remaining thing to check being that these operations preserve the tautological ring.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

In addition to its cup product, there is a cocommutative coproduct and an endomorphism

\oplus^{*}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}},\hskip 42.67912ptT\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}

making
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
into a
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
-linear commutative, cocommutative bialgebra with biderivation.

Proof.

Since
\oplus^{*}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{E}_{i}\boxtimes 1+1\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\textup{act}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{i}=\gamma\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i}
, the maps on cohomology defined in Lemma
2.2.2
send

\displaystyle\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\mapsto}\limits}}\ \textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E})\otimes 1+1\otimes\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i}),

\displaystyle\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \textup{ch}_{r}(\gamma\boxtimes\mathcal{E}_{i})\ =\ \sum\frac{1}{k!}z^{k}\textup{ch}_{r-k}(\mathcal{E}_{i})

by section
A.2
, and so
\textup{act}_{z}^{*}=\exp(Tz)
where
T
sends
\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})\mapsto\textup{ch}_{r-1}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
for positive
r
. Both maps therefore preserve the subspaces of tautological classes.
∎

Corollary 4.3.3
.

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
is a holomorphic
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
-linear vertex bialgebra with vertex coproduct
\textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}\circ\oplus^{*}

Proof.

This follows from the standard equivalence between holomorphic vertex bialgebras and bialgebras with biderivation extending Lemma
1.6.4
. This equivalence sends the coproduct
\Delta
and coderivation
T
to
\Delta(z)=(e^{zT}\otimes\textup{id})\Delta
, which in this case is
\oplus^{*}(z)=\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}
.
∎

4.3.4.
Description as a colimit

Let
\textbf{{C}}^{e}
denote the
trivial
representation of
Q
with dimension
e\in\Lambda
, all of whose edge maps are zero. Taking the direct sum with this representation induces a map of stacks

(44)

\iota_{d,e}\ :\ \mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T}\ \to\ \mathcal{M}_{Q,d+e}^{T},

induced by the associated map on the representation vector spaces

\textup{Rep}(Q,d)\ \hookrightarrow\ \textup{Rep}(Q,d+e),\hskip 42.67912ptV\ \mapsto\ V\oplus\textbf{{C}}^{e}

which intertwines the actions of
\textup{GL}_{d}\hookrightarrow\textup{GL}_{d+e}
. We thus have a diagram of stacks indexed by a poset

(\Lambda,\leq)\ \to\ \textup{Stk},\hskip 42.67912ptd\leq d^{\prime}\ \ \mapsto\ \ \mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d}\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d^{\prime}},

and can identify the tautological ring as the associated limit on cohomology. Writing
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}^{+}
for the subring generated by positive rank chern classes, We have

(45)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}^{+}\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \lim_{d\in\Lambda}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})

is the limit of the above diagram as a graded algebra. In particular, the tautological ring should loosely speaking be viewed as the
T
-equivariant cohomology of the limit of the above functor

\mathcal{M}_{Q,\infty}\ \vcentcolon=\ \textup{colim}_{d\in\Lambda}\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}\ \simeq\ \textup{Rep}(Q,\infty)/\textup{GL}_{\infty}

where
\textup{Rep}(Q,\infty)=\bigcup_{d}\textup{Rep}(Q,d)
and
\textup{GL}_{\infty}=\bigcup_{d}\textup{GL}_{d}
are the unions of the representation spaces and groups under the above-defined maps, and the second isomorphism holds since quotient stacks are defined as colimits and any two colimits commute.

4.4.
Braidings and bialgebras

We start this section with making precise exactly in what sense the products and coproducts we consider will end up being compatible. The set up here is used in the main Theorem
4.5.2
of this section.

4.4.1.

A bialgebra is a vector space
A
equipped with an algebra structure and a coalgebra structure such that

(46)

\Delta(a\cdot b)\ =\ \Delta(a)\cdot_{\beta}\Delta(b),

where the right hand side means we swap the middle two factors using the braiding before multiplying:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes A)\otimes(A\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes A)\otimes(A\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\otimes\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes m}

To be able to define an algebra or coalgebra, we have to work in a monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes)
. To be able to define a bialgebra, we have to work in a braided monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\beta)
. Since our objects of interest are vertex and localised bialgebras, we need to work with meromorphic and localised braided monoidal categories.

4.4.2.

There exist several notions of meromorphic braided monoidal category in the literature, see for example
[
35
,
52
]
. However, in this paper we do not try to put our results into this categorical context, instead we define everything in terms of explicit maps

\beta_{M,N}(z)\ :\ M\otimes N((z^{-1}))\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ N\otimes M((z^{-1})),\hskip 42.67912pt\beta_{M,N,\textup{loc}}\ :\ M\otimes N[S^{-1}]\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ N\otimes M[S^{-1}]

for any pair of objects
M,N
, satisfying the hexagon relations with spectral parameter:

(47)

\beta_{L\otimes M((w^{-1})),N}(z)\ =\ \beta_{L,N}(z-w)\beta_{M,N}(z),\hskip 42.67912pt\beta_{L,M\otimes N((w^{-1}))}(z)\ =\ \beta_{L,M}(z+w)\beta_{L,N}(z),

and hence the meromorphic braid relation:

(48)

\beta_{L,M}(z)\beta_{L,N}(z+w)\beta_{M,N}(w)\ =\ \beta_{M,N}(w)\beta_{L,N}(z+w)\beta_{L,M}(z).

and likewise for
\beta_{\textup{loc}}
, defined in subsection
4.4.10
.

4.4.3.
Tautological ring modules

We will consider meromorphic braidings on the category of tautological modules.
We first consider the properties of the tautological part of the
R
-matrix for a general quiver.

Firstly a warning: the tautological part of the
R
-matrix does
not
satisfy the spectral hexagon relations. The failure is measured by the antisymmetrisation
\widetilde{\chi}(d_{1},d_{2})=\chi(d_{1},d_{2})-\chi(d_{2},d_{1})
of the Euler form:

Lemma 4.4.4
.

R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
satisfies

(49)

\displaystyle(\oplus^{*}(w)\,\otimes\,\textup{id})R_{\textup{taut}}(z)_{d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}

\displaystyle\ =\ \frac{(z-w)^{\widetilde{\chi}(d_{3},d_{1})}}{z^{\widetilde{\chi}(d_{3},d_{1})}}R_{\textup{taut},13}(z-w)_{d_{1},d_{3}}R_{\textup{taut},23}(z)_{d_{2},d_{3}},

(50)

\displaystyle(\textup{id}\,\otimes\,\oplus^{*}(w))R_{\textup{taut}}(z)_{d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}

\displaystyle\ =\ \frac{(z+w)^{\widetilde{\chi}(d_{2},d_{1})}}{z^{\widetilde{\chi}(d_{2},d_{1})}}R_{\textup{taut},12}(z+w)_{d_{1},d_{2}}R_{\textup{taut},13}(z)_{d_{1},d_{3}}.

Proof.

We prove this using a combination of

(51)

\displaystyle(\oplus^{*}\otimes\textup{id})R_{\textup{taut},d_{1}+d_{2},d_{3}}(z)\

\displaystyle=\ R_{\textup{taut},d_{1},d_{3}}(z)R_{\textup{taut},23,d_{2},d_{3}}(z)

\displaystyle(\textup{id}\otimes\oplus^{*})R_{\textup{taut},d_{1},d_{2}+d_{3}}(z)\

\displaystyle=\ R_{\textup{taut},d_{1},d_{2}}(z)R_{\textup{taut},13,d_{1},d_{3}}(z)

\displaystyle\textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}R_{\textup{taut},d_{1},d_{2}}(z)\

\displaystyle=\ \left(\frac{z-w}{z}\right)^{\widetilde{\chi}(d_{2},d_{1})}R_{\textup{taut}}(z-w)

(52)

\displaystyle\textup{act}_{w,2}^{*}R_{\textup{taut},d_{1},d_{2}}(z)\

\displaystyle=\ \left(\frac{z+w}{z}\right)^{\widetilde{\chi}(d_{2},d_{1})}R_{\textup{taut}}(z+w)

by the definition of
\oplus^{*}(w)
. Note that
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)=\bigsqcap c(\mathcal{E},z)^{\pm}
is an alternating product of
c(\mathcal{E},z)
’s over a set of vector bundles
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
satisfying

(\oplus^{*}\,\otimes\,\textup{id})\mathcal{E}\ =\ \mathcal{E}_{13}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{23},\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{act}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{E}\ =\ \gamma^{-1}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}

(\textup{id}\,\otimes\,\oplus^{*})\mathcal{E}\ =\ \mathcal{E}_{12}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{13},\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{act}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{E}\ =\ \gamma\boxtimes\mathcal{E}.

The result thus follows for
c(\mathcal{E},z)
by Proposition
A.2.5
, and hence for
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
.
∎

Using the relation between the full and tautological parts
4.2.3
we immediately get

Corollary 4.4.5
.

The full Joyce
R
-matrix satisfies the spectral hexagon relations

(\oplus^{*}(w)\otimes\textup{id})R(z)\ =\ R(z-w)_{13}R(z)_{23},\hskip 28.45274pt(\textup{id}\otimes\oplus^{*}(w))R(z)\ =\ R(z)_{13}R(z+w)_{13}

and
\sigma(R(z))
satisfies the same, with
w
negated on the right hand side of both equations.

Corollary 4.4.6
.

Let
Q
be symmetric, then
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
is a quasitriangular vertex bialgebra: a vertex bialgebra with an element
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
satisfying Lemma
4.4.4
and

\sigma(z)\cdot\oplus^{*}(\textup{act}^{*}_{z}h,-z)\ =\ R_{\textup{taut}}(z)^{-1}\oplus^{*}(h,z)R_{\textup{taut}}(z).

Proof.

The
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
-conjugation on the right side is trivial since the cup product on even degree classes is commutative. We finish by noting that

\sigma\textup{act}_{-z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\ =\ \sigma\cdot\textup{act}_{z,2}^{*}\oplus^{*}\ =\ \textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}

which implies that
\sigma\oplus^{*}(\textup{act}^{*}_{z}h,-z)=\oplus^{*}(h,z)
as required.
∎

Let
Q
be symmetric, so that
R(z)=R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
is
both
tautological-valued and satisfies the spectral hexagon relations. The category on which we will consider a meromorphic braiding is

\mathcal{C}\ =\ H\text{-}\textup{Mod}(\textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda}),

the category of modules over the tautological ring
H=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
. Here

\textup{Vect}_{\Lambda}^{T}\ =\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)\text{-}\textup{Mod}_{\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}}

is the category of
\Lambda
-graded vector spaces with an additional “cohomological”
Z
-grading denoted by
|\ \ |
and action of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
. Both categories are equipped with monoidal structure
\otimes_{T}
as defined in (
18
).
The
meromorphic braiding
on
(\mathcal{C},\otimes_{T})
is given by

(53)

\displaystyle\beta(z)\ :\ V_{\lambda}\otimes_{T}W_{\mu}((z^{-1}))

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ W_{\mu}\otimes_{T}V_{\lambda}((z^{-1}))

\displaystyle v\otimes w

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \sigma\cdot\left(R(z)\cdot v\otimes w\right)

where
\sigma
is the Koszul sign braiding and we have braided vector spaces concentrated in degree
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
. Here we view (
53
) as a map of modules over the tautological ring, where a tautological class
h
acts on both via the holomorphic vertex coproduct
\oplus^{*}(h,z)=\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}(h)
. This defines a meromorphic braiding as a consequence of the above corollaries.

4.4.7.
Vertex bialgebras

Given such a braiding as above, we define

Definition 4.4.8
.

A
vertex bialgebra
is an object
B
with an associative product
m
and coassociative vertex coproduct
\Delta(z)
, such that

(54)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{T}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes_{T}B)\otimes_{T}(B\otimes_{T}B)((z^{-1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes_{T}B)\otimes_{T}(B\otimes_{T}B)((z^{-1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{T}B((z^{-1}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(z)\otimes_{T}\Delta(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{23}(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes_{T}m}

commutes.

Recalling the definition of localised coproduct from section
3.1
,

Definition 4.4.9
.

A
localised bialgebra
is a graded object
B=(B_{\alpha})
with translation equivariant localised coproduct and associative graded product

m\ :\ B_{\alpha}\otimes B_{\beta}\ \to\ B_{\alpha+\beta}

linear over
\mathcal{O}
, such that

(55)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{T}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes_{T}B)\otimes_{T}(B\otimes_{T}B)_{(12,14,32,34)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes_{T}B)\otimes_{T}(B\otimes_{T}B)_{(13,14,23,24)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes_{T}B)_{(12)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\cdot\Delta_{\textup{loc}}\otimes_{T}\Delta_{\textup{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\textup{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\textup{loc},23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes_{T}m}

commutes, where
u
is the inclusion into the localisation by the
(14,32)
-factors.

In the above, we have extended
m\otimes_{T}m
to a map on the localisation

B^{\otimes_{T}4}_{(13,23)}\ \simeq\ B^{\otimes_{T}4}[(\delta\times\delta)^{*}S^{-1}]\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ B^{\otimes_{T}2}[S^{-1}]\ =\ B^{\otimes_{T}2}_{(12)}

where the first isomorphism is given by applying (
34
) to give that the localisations by
S_{13}S_{14}S_{23}S_{24}
and
(\delta\times\delta)^{*}S
are isomorphic, and the middle map is induced by
m\otimes_{T}m
by
\mathcal{O}
-linearity of
m\otimes_{T}m
.

4.4.10.
Localised structures

We now specialise to the case of cohomology of quiver moduli stacks as in subsection
3.2
. Given a collection of
Z
-graded modules
V_{\alpha_{i}}
over the cohomology rings
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d_{i}})
, we define their
localised monoidal product
by

\left(V_{d_{1}}\otimes_{T}V_{d_{2}}\right)_{(12)}\ =\ V_{d_{1}}\otimes_{T}V_{d_{2}}[S_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{-1}]

and likewise for iterated tensor products:

(56)

\left(V_{d_{1}}\otimes_{T}\cdots\otimes_{T}V_{d_{n}}\right)_{(i_{1}j_{1},i_{2}j_{2},\ldots\ )}\ =\ \left(V_{d_{1}}\otimes_{T}\cdots\otimes_{T}V_{d_{n}}\right)\left[S_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{-1},S_{i_{2}j_{2}}^{-1},\cdots\ \right].

The
localised braiding
on
\otimes_{T}
is

(57)

\displaystyle\beta_{\textup{loc}}\ :\ (V_{\alpha}\otimes_{T}W_{\beta})_{(12)}

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ (W_{\beta}\otimes_{T}V_{\alpha})_{(21)}

\displaystyle v\otimes w

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \sigma\cdot\left(R_{\textup{loc}}\cdot v\otimes w\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ (-1)^{|v|\cdot|w|}\sigma(R_{\textup{loc}})\cdot w\otimes v

where

R_{\textup{loc}}\ =\ \frac{e(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee})}{e(\textup{Ext})}\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}}^{T})\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{2}}^{T})[S_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{-1}]

is the
localised Joyce
R
-matrix
. We emphasise that we
cannot
assemble this into an element of a localisation of the tautological ring, see Warning
4.1.4
.

Lemma 4.4.11
.

We have
R_{\textup{loc}}=\frac{e(Q_{1})}{e(Q_{1}^{\textup{op}})}
.

Proof.

We compute

R_{\textup{loc}}\ =\ \frac{e(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}_{0}^{\vee})e(\textup{Ext}_{1})}{e(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}_{1}^{\vee})e(\textup{Ext}_{0})}\ =\ \frac{e(\textup{Ext}_{1})}{e(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}_{1}^{\vee})}\ =\ \frac{e(Q_{1})}{e(Q_{1}^{\textup{op}})}

where in the second equality we used that
\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}_{0}^{\vee}\simeq\textup{Ext}_{0}
, and in the third Proposition
1.4.3
.
∎

Lemma 4.4.12
.

The full Joyce and localised Joyce
R
-matrices are compatible:
(\textup{id}\otimes\textup{act}_{z}^{*})(R_{\textup{loc}})=R(z)
.

Proof.

Follows from Proposition
A.2.10
.
∎

Corollary 4.4.13
.

\textup{act}_{2}\cdot\beta_{\textup{loc}}=\beta(z)\cdot\textup{act}_{1}
.

4.4.14.
Explicit formula for full Joyce
R
-matrix

We can now explicitly compute the full Joyce
R
-matrix in terms of chern roots of tautological bundles

Proposition 4.4.15
.

We have that

(58)

R(z)_{d,d^{{}^{\prime}}}\ =\bigsqcap_{e\,:\,i\to j}\frac{\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d_{i},d_{j}^{\prime})}(1\otimes x_{j,m}-x_{i,n}\otimes 1+z+\textup{wt}(e))}{\bigsqcap_{(n,m)=(1,1)}^{(d_{j},d_{i}^{\prime})}(1\otimes x_{i,m}-x_{j,n}\otimes 1+z-\textup{wt}(e))}

viewed as an element of

\textup{Sym}_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\left(x_{i,\alpha}\ :\ i\in Q_{0},\,0\leq\alpha\leq d_{1,i}\right)\otimes_{T}\textup{Sym}_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}\left(x_{i,\beta}\ :\ i\in Q_{0},\,0\leq\beta\leq d_{2,i}\right)((z^{-1})).

Proof.

This is a combination of the Lemmas
4.4.11
,
4.4.12
, Proposition
1.4.3
and the fact that
\textup{act}^{*}
sends a chern root of a tautological bundle
x
to
x+z
.
∎

4.4.16.
Sign twists

Consider the
sign twist
map

\displaystyle\tau\ :\ \textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}\times\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}

\displaystyle\ \to\ \textbf{{Z}}/2

\displaystyle(d_{1},d_{2})

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \chi(d_{1},d_{1})\chi(d_{2},d_{2})+\chi(d_{1},d_{2}).

We define the twisted meromorphic and localised braidings by

(59)

\beta^{\tau}(z)\ =\ (-1)^{\tau}\beta(z),\hskip 42.67912pt\beta_{\textup{loc}}^{\tau}\ =\ (-1)^{\tau}\beta_{\textup{loc}}.

Thus the sign twisted localised braiding
\beta_{\textup{loc}}^{\tau}
agrees with
\widetilde{\textup{sw}}^{\tau}=\sigma\left((-1)^{\tau}\frac{e(Q_{1}^{\textup{op}})}{e(Q_{1})}\cdot(-)\right)
in the notation of
[
5
, Section 4.1]
. Note in
[
5
]

\widetilde{\textup{sw}}
is written already containing
\tau
but we consider both with and without the
\tau
sign twist.
This will become useful because the ordinary CoHA product only forms a bialgebra for the
\tau
-twisted braidings, although to match up with Yangians we precompose the CoHA product with another sign twist
\psi
,
giving a bialgebra with respect to the untwisted braidings.

Consider any

\psi\ :\ (\textbf{{Z}}/2)^{Q_{0}}\times(\textbf{{Z}}/2)^{Q_{0}}\ \to\ \textbf{{Z}}/2

satisfying
\psi(d_{1},d_{2})+\psi(d_{2},d_{1})=\tau(d_{1},d_{2})
. Then by
[
9
, Thm.5.13]
and
[
5
, Prop. 5.5]
in the
T
equivariant setting, the diagram

(60)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W})\otimes_{T}(\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W})_{(12,14,32,34)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W})\otimes_{T}(\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W})_{(13,14,23,24)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W})_{(12)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{(\psi)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\cdot\Delta_{\textup{loc}}\otimes_{T}\Delta_{\textup{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\textup{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\textup{sw}}^{(\tau)}_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{(\psi)}\otimes_{T}m^{(\psi)}}

commutes, where
u
is the map into the further localisation by the
(14,32)
-factors, and where we are allowed to move the sign factor onto either the braiding or the multiplication, i.e. we have

(\widetilde{\textup{sw}}^{(\tau)},m^{(\psi)})\ =\ (\widetilde{\textup{sw}}^{\tau},m),\,\textup{or}\,(\widetilde{\textup{sw}},m^{\psi}=m\cdot(-1)^{\psi}).

We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
the vector space
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
but with the twisted version

(61)

m^{\psi}\ =\ m\cdot(-1)^{\psi}

of the CoHA product
m
defined in subsection
1.5
.

4.5.
The CoHA is a vertex bialgebra

4.5.1.

We come to the first main result of the paper about arbitrary quivers
Q
with potential
W
, graded over the character lattice of a torus
T
. See the sections
0.5.2
and
0.5.4
for motivations from the theory of quantum groups and physics for why one should have expected this structure. We begin with the symmetric case.

Theorem 4.5.2
.

Let
Q
be symmetric.
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
is a vertex bialgebra inside
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\text{-}\textup{Mod}(\textup{Vect}_{\Lambda}^{T})
. Its Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct
\Delta(z)
is braided colocal.

Unwinding the Definition
4.4.8
of vertex bialgebra, this means the following:

(1)

Firstly
B=\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
is an element of this category: it is a
\Lambda
-graded vector space with an action of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
, a cohomological
Z
-grading, and moreover an action of the tautological ring by cup product, which we denote by

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}.

(2)

Tautological ring
.
H=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
is a commutative, cocommutative bialgebra with its cup product and
\oplus^{*}
coproduct. It has a
\textbf{{C}}[z]
-coaction
\textup{act}^{*}:H\to H[z]
and hence forms a holomorphic vertex bialgebra with vertex coproduct
\oplus^{*}(z)=\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}
. There is an element

R(z)\ \in\ H\otimes H((z^{-1}))

satisfying the spectral hexagon relations for
\oplus^{*}(w)
as in Lemma
4.4.4
.

We can thus define bialgebras in
H\text{-}\textup{Mod}
as in Definition
4.4.8
.

(3)

CoHA and cup product
.
B=\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
has an associative product
\star:B\otimes_{T}B\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}B
which is linear over the cup product action of
H
:

(62)

m^{\psi}(\oplus^{*}(h)\cdot(b\otimes b^{\prime}))\ =\ h\cdot m^{\psi}(b\otimes b^{\prime}).

Thus
B
is an associative algebra internal to this category.

(4)

\Delta(z)
and cup product
. There is a coassociative vertex coproduct
\Delta(z):B\to B\otimes_{T}B((z^{-1}))
which is linear over the cup product action of
H
:

(63)

\Delta(h\cdot b,z)\ =\ \oplus^{*}(h,z)\cdot\Delta(b,z).

Thus
B
is a coassociative vertex coalgebra internal to this category.

(5)

CoHA and
\Delta(z)
form a vertex bialgebra
for the meromorphic braiding
\beta(z)=\sigma(R(z)\cdot(-))
as in Definition

4.4.8
:

(64)

\Delta(b\star b^{\prime},z)\ =\ \Delta(b,z)\star_{R(z)}\Delta(b^{\prime},z),

(6)

Braided colocality
. The Joyce–Liu coproduct is cocommutative up to the meromorphic braiding:

(65)

\Delta_{d_{1},d_{2}}(z)=(-1)^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}_{d_{1},d_{2}}}\sigma_{d_{1},d_{2}}\cdot R_{d_{2},d_{1}}(z)\Delta_{d_{2},d_{1}}(-z)e^{zT}

The same Theorem is true for the untwisted CoHA
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
, except we use the
\tau
-twisted meromorphic braiding
\beta^{\tau}(z)
. Most proofs of properties such as (
64
) work by a torus localisation argument, but we sidestep this by using Davisons’ version
[
9
]
of this statement in the localised case.

Corollary 4.5.3
.

If
Q
is graded symmetric, then
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
is a
\Lambda\times\textbf{{Z}}
-graded
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
-linear vertex bialgebra, where colocality is satisfied up to a sign depending on the Euler form of
Q
.

Proof.

When the Euler form
\chi
is symmetric, the vertex coproduct becomes

\Delta_{\alpha+\beta}(z)\ :\ B_{\alpha+\beta}\ \to\ B_{\alpha}\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)}B_{\beta}((z^{-1}))

without a cohomological shift by the definition (
18
) of
\otimes_{T}
, and since
R(z)=1\otimes 1
the braiding becomes
\beta(z)=\sigma
the Koszul sign rule braiding, so colocality

\Delta(\alpha,z)\ =\ (-1)^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}}\sigma(\Delta(\alpha,-z)e^{zT})

becomes ordinary (super)colocality up to sign.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.5.2
.

All structures (
2
) on the tautological ring were proven in section
4.3
. On
B=\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
, we let
m^{\psi}
be the sign-twisted associative CoHA product (
61
) and
\Delta(z)
the Joyce–Liu coassociative vertex coproduct of Theorem
2.3.1
.

Compatibility of CoHA with cup product
(
3
). We compute

\displaystyle p_{*}q^{*}(\oplus^{*}(h)\cup(b\otimes b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=p_{*}(q^{*}\oplus^{*}h\cup q^{*}(b\otimes b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=p_{*}(q^{*}(p\cdot s)^{*}h\cup q^{*}(b\otimes b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=p_{*}(q^{*}s^{*}p^{*}h\cup q^{*}(b\otimes b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=p_{*}(p^{*}h\cup q^{*}(b\otimes b^{\prime}))\quad\text{projection formula \eqref{eqn:ProjectionFormulaVanishing}}

\displaystyle=h\cup p_{*}q^{*}(b\otimes b^{\prime}).

Compatibility of
\Delta(z)
with cup product
(
4
). We use Lemma
2.2.4
, which also works for the tautological ring, to compute

\displaystyle\Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{1}(\oplus^{*}(\alpha\cdot\beta))

\displaystyle=\Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{1}(\oplus^{*}(\alpha)\cdot\oplus^{*}(\beta))

\displaystyle=\Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{1}(\oplus^{*}(\alpha))\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{1}(\oplus^{*}(\beta))

\displaystyle=\textup{act}^{*}_{1}(\oplus^{*}(\alpha))\Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\cdot

\displaystyle=\oplus^{*}(\alpha,z)\cdot\Delta(\beta,z).

Compatibility of
\Delta(z)
and the CoHA
(
64
). We use Davisons’ result (
60
) that
B=\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
forms a localised bialgebra, i.e. that the inner face of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{T}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{T}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes_{T}B)\otimes_{T}(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes_{T}B)_{(12,14,32,34)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes_{T}B)\otimes_{T}(B\otimes_{T}B)((z^{-1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes_{T}\boldsymbol{B})\otimes_{T}(B\otimes_{T}B)_{(13,14,23,24)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes_{T}B)_{(12)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes_{T}B)\otimes_{T}(B\otimes_{T}B)((z^{-1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes_{T}B((z^{-1}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(z)\otimes_{T}\Delta(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\cdot\Delta_{\textup{loc}}\otimes_{T}\Delta_{\textup{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\textup{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\textup{loc}\textup{,}23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{act}_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{23}(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes_{T}m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{act}_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{act}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes_{T}m}

commutes. Here, the arrows are the
loc
maps from Definition
3.1.5
induced by
\textup{act}^{*}
acting on the copies of
B
drawn in bold, and the subscript
(ij)=[S_{ij}^{-1}]
means a localisation along a copy of
S
acting on the
ij
th tensor factors as in section
4.4.10
.

The remaining five faces commute for the following reasons. The top face is the identity, and the bottom face commutes by linearity of the CoHA product over action by the cup product. Note we can commute the map
\textup{act}_{1,3}
past the localisation map
u
because the map
\textup{act}_{1,3}
, applied to the appropriate classes we need to localise, will be invertible. The remaining faces commute because

\Delta(z)\cdot\textup{act}\ =\ \textup{act}\cdot\Delta_{\textup{loc}},\hskip 42.67912pt\beta(z)\cdot\textup{act}\ =\ \textup{act}\cdot\beta_{\textup{loc}}

by Theorem
3.3.2
and Corollary
4.4.13
. Thus the outer face commutes, and
B
forms a vertex bialgebra.

Braided colocality
(
6
). We have that

\displaystyle\Delta(\alpha,z)

\displaystyle\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\ =\ \sigma\left(\Psi(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext},-z)\textup{act}_{z,2}^{*}\oplus^{*}(\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ \sigma\left(\frac{\Psi(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext},-z)}{\Psi(\textup{Ext},z)}\cdot\Psi(\textup{Ext},z)\textup{act}_{-z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}(\alpha)\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ (-1)^{\textup{rk}\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}}\sigma\left(R(z)\cdot\Delta(\alpha,-z)e^{zT}\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ (-1)^{\textup{rk}\textup{Ext}}\beta(z)\cdot\Delta(\alpha,-z)e^{zT}

where in the second equality we used that
\oplus^{*}
is cocommutative and in the third equality we used the fact that

\textup{act}_{z,2}^{*}\oplus^{*}\ =\ \textup{act}_{-z,1}^{*}\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\textup{act}_{z,2}^{*}\oplus^{*}\ =\ \textup{act}_{-z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}\textup{act}_{z}^{*}.

This shows braided colocality.

∎

4.5.4.
Non-symmetric case

If
Q
is not symmetric, we do not currently have a good generalisation
H^{\prime}
of the tautological ring with an element
R^{\prime}(z)\in H\otimes H((z^{-1}))
satisfying the spectral hexagon relations. Indeed, if we took
H^{\prime}=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
itself then
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
does not in general satisfy the spectral hexagon relations, and
R(z)\not\in H\otimes H((z^{-1}))
.

We do not address this question in this paper.

As a stopgap, we can consider the category

\mathcal{C}=\bigsqcap_{d\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T})\text{-}\textup{Mod}
whose objects consist of a collection
V=(V_{d})
of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T})
-modules for every dimension vector
d
. There is a monoidal structure

(V\otimes_{T}W)_{d}\ =\ \bigoplus_{d=d_{1}+d_{2}}V_{d_{1}}\otimes_{T}W_{d_{2}}

which is additive on dimension vectors, and
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d_{1}+d_{2}}^{T})
acts on the tensor via the direct sum pullback
\oplus^{*}
. The monoidal structure
\otimes_{T}
has a natural meromorphic braiding given by

\displaystyle\beta(z)\ :\ V_{d}\otimes_{T}W_{d^{\prime}}((z^{-1}))

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ W_{d^{\prime}}\otimes_{T}V_{d}((z^{-1}))

\displaystyle v\otimes w

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \sigma(R(z)_{d,d^{\prime}}\cdot v\otimes w).

We may view the CoHA as an element
B=(\mathcal{A}_{Q,W,d}^{T,\psi})_{d\in\textbf{{N}}^{Q_{0}}}
of this category.

Thus–until we have a good analogue of the tautological ring for nonsymmetric quivers–the best analogue of Theorem
4.5.2
we have is

Theorem 4.5.5
.

Let
(Q,W)
be an arbitrary quiver with potential acted on by torus
T
leaving the potential invariant, and satisfying the Künneth assumption (
31
). The CoHA
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
is a vertex bialgebra inside the category
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The same as for
4.5.2
.
∎

Note however that because
\mathcal{C}
is not a module category, we cannot apply bosonisation in the sense of next section to add a Cartan piece to
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
. See subsection
5.3.3
for more discussion.

5.
Extending CoHAs and bosonisation

In this section, we explain how to extend CoHAs systematically. In the case of any symmetric
N
-graded quiver
Q
with potential
W
we will define the
extended CoHA

\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi,\textup{ext}}_{Q,W}\ =\ \mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}

and show that it inherits algebraic structures from
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
. We prove the following in section
5.3.1
:

Theorem 5.0.1
.

Let
Q
be a symmetric quiver. The extended CoHA has an associative product and nonlocal vertex coproduct

(66)

\displaystyle b\otimes h\,\cdot\,b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ =\ \left[b\cdot((\oplus^{*}h)_{(1)}\cup b^{\prime})\right]\otimes\left[(\oplus^{*}h)_{(2)}\cup h^{\prime}\right]

(67)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b\otimes h,z)

\displaystyle\ =\ R_{\textup{taut}}(z)_{32}\cup\left(\Delta(b,z)_{13}\otimes(\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}h)_{24}\right)

endowing it with the structure of a vertex bialgebra inside
\textup{Vect}_{\Lambda}^{T}
.

This statement and its proof is an application of a much more general phenomenon that we call vertex Majid–Radford bosonisation (Theorem
5.2.2
). In pictures, the extended product and coproduct are
\scriptstyle\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
\scriptstyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
\scriptstyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
\scriptstyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
\scriptstyle R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
\scriptstyle\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
\scriptstyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
\scriptstyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
\scriptstyle\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
\scriptstyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}

where merging lines corresponds to the CoHA product or cup product, and splitting lines correspond to the Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct
\Delta(z)
or
\oplus^{*}
. See
[
19
]
for an introduction to string diagrams for quantum groups.

5.0.2.
Remark

The above Theorem also holds for the untwisted CoHA
\mathcal{A}^{T}_{Q,W}
and
(-1)^{\tau}R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
.

5.1.
Majid–Radford bosonisation

5.1.1.

Majid
[
42
]
and Radford
[
44
]
noticed that if
B
is a vector space with a (right) action of associative algebra
H
, then additional algebraic structures from
H,B
induce analogous structures on the
bosonisation

B\rtimes H=B\otimes H
:

Theorem 5.1.2
.

[
41
, Thm. 4.1]

If
H
is a bialgebra and
B
is an
H
-linear algebra, then

(68)

(b\otimes h)\cdot(b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime})\ =\ b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})\otimes h_{(2)}h^{\prime}

makes
B\rtimes H
into an associative algebra. If additionally
H
has a quasitriangular element
R=R^{(1)}\otimes R^{(2)}\in H\otimes H
and
B
is a bialgebra in
H\text{-}\textup{Mod}
with respect to the induced braiding, then

(69)

\Delta(b\otimes h)\ =\ (b_{(1)}\otimes R^{(2)}h_{(1)})\otimes(R^{(1)}\cdot b_{(2)}\otimes h_{(2)})

makes
B\rtimes H
into a bialgebra.

This is obvious from a module category perspective. Indeed, applying Barr–Beck to the forgetful functor
\textup{oblv}:B\text{-}\textup{Mod}(H\text{-}\textup{Mod})\to\textup{Vect}
induces an equivalence

(70)

B\text{-}\textup{Mod}(H\text{-}\textup{Mod})\ \simeq\ (B\rtimes H)\text{-}\textup{Mod}

for
some
algebra
B\rtimes H=\textup{End}(\textup{oblv})
. An object in (
70
) is a vector space together with right
H
- and
B
-actions satisfying certain commutation relations determined by the action of
H
on
B
. Therefore as a vector space we have
B\rtimes H=B\otimes H
, whose algebra structure (
68
) is fixed by the commutation relations and the observation that
B,H
are subalgebras.

Noticing that a monoidal structure on
A\text{-}\textup{Mod}
lifting that of
Vect
is precisely equivalent to a bialgebra structure on
A
, the coproduct (
69
) on
B\rtimes H
is then fixed by asking that the equivalence (
70
) is monoidal.

5.1.3.

In summary, the following structures on
H
and
B
induce the following structures on the bosonisation:

if

H

B

then

B\rtimes H

has a

product

H
-action

–

–

and

coproduct

product

has a

product

and

quasitriangular

coproduct

and

coproduct

element
R

and we may choose to take the vertex algebra analogue of any row(s). In what follows we will need the last:

if

H

B

then

B\rtimes H

has a

product

H
-action

–

–

and

coproduct

product

has a

product

and

spectral

vertex

and

vertex

quasitriangular

coproduct

coproduct

element
R(z)

where now all vector spaces are also endowed with “translation” endomorphisms respecting the above structures. We should view Theorem
5.2.2
as saying that we have an equivalence

B\text{-}\textup{Mod}(H\text{-}\textup{Mod})\ \simeq\ B\rtimes H\text{-}\textup{Mod}

which moreover respects the meromorphic tensor structures on both sides, although as mentioned we do not make this precise.

5.1.4.
Remark

In the presence of a Tannakian reconstruction statement for meromorphic monoidal categories we could view
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi,\textup{ext}}_{Q,W}\in\textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda}
as the endomorphism algebra of the forgetful functor

(71)

\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}\text{-}\textup{Mod}\left(\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\text{-}\textup{Mod}(\textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda})\right)\ \to\ \textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda}

so that
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi,\textup{ext}}_{Q,W}\text{-}\textup{Mod}(\textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda})
would be equivalent to the left side of (
71
) and the vertex coproduct is inherited from structure on that category.

5.2.
Vertex bosonisation

5.2.1.

Consider vector spaces
H,B
and
R(z)\in H\otimes H((z^{-1}))
satisfying the axioms (
1
)-(
64
) listed below Theorem
4.5.2
.
For simplicity, we will also assume that the product on
H
is commutative.

In the following we use the notation for
R
-matrices

R(z)\ =\ R^{(1)}(z)\otimes R^{(2)}(z),

and the Sweedler notation for coproducts and vertex coproducts

\Delta(a)\ =\ a_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)},\hskip 42.67912pt\Delta(a,z)\ =\ a_{(1,z)}\otimes a_{(2,z)},

where in both cases the summation is implied. As with usual Sweedler notation, the term
a_{(1,z)}
will never appear alone without
a_{(2,z)}
.

Theorem 5.2.2
.

The vector space
B\rtimes H=B\otimes H
with product

(72)

(b\otimes h)\cdot(b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime})\ =\ b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})\otimes h_{(2)}h^{\prime}

is an associative algebra, and the (nonlocal) vertex coproduct

(73)

\Delta(b\otimes h,z)\ =\ (b_{(1,z)}\otimes R^{(2)}(z)h_{(1,z)})\otimes(R^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2,z)})

makes
B\rtimes H
into a nonlocal vertex bialgebra.

Remark 5.2.3
.

There are three proofs of this fact one could give: first by arduous explicit computations as below, and second by an equally arduous string diagram argument. The cleanest way is to deduce it formally from ordinary Majid–Radford bosonisation, by noticing that vertex bialgebras are equivalent to bialgebras in a certain category of sheaves on a Ran space, and likewise for the other structures appearing above, see e.g.
[
35
]
.

Proof of Theorem
5.2.2
.

Associativity.

\displaystyle\left((b\otimes h)\cdot(b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime})\right)\cdot(b^{\prime\prime}\otimes h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\ =\ \left(b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})\otimes h_{(2)}h^{\prime}\right)\cdot(b^{\prime\prime}\otimes h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\ =\ b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})((h_{(2)}h^{\prime})_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime\prime})\otimes(h_{2}h^{\prime})_{(2)}h^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\ =\ b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})(h_{(2)(1)}h^{\prime}_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime\prime})\otimes h_{(2)(2)}h^{\prime}_{(2)}h^{\prime\prime}

H
is a bialgebra

\displaystyle\ =\ b(h_{(1)(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})(h_{(1)(2)}h^{\prime}_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime\prime})\otimes h_{(2)}h^{\prime}_{(2)}h^{\prime\prime}

coassociativity of
H

\displaystyle\ =\ b\left(h_{(1)}\cdot(b^{\prime}(h^{\prime}_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime\prime}))\right)\cdot h_{(2)}h^{\prime}_{(2)}h^{\prime\prime}

H
-linearity of
B
’s product

\displaystyle\ =\ (b\otimes h)\cdot\left(b^{\prime}(h^{\prime}_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime\prime})\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2)}h^{\prime\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ (b\otimes h)\cdot\left((b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime})\cdot(b^{\prime\prime}\otimes h^{\prime\prime})\right)

Coassociativity.

We prove vertex coassociativity

(\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(z)\otimes\textup{id})\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(w)\ =\ (\textup{id}\otimes\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(w))\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(z+w)

by computing both sides:

\displaystyle\left(\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(z)\otimes\textup{id}\right)\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b\otimes h,w)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left(\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(z)\otimes\textup{id}\right)\left(b_{(1,w)}\otimes R^{(2)}(w)h_{(1,w)})\otimes(R^{(1)}(w)\cdot b_{(2,w)}\otimes h_{(2,w)})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,w)(1,z)}\otimes\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)(R^{(2)}(w)h_{(1,w)})_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(1,w)(2,z)}\otimes(R^{(2)}(w)h_{(1,w)})_{(2,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\otimes\left[R^{(1)}(w)\cdot b_{(2,w)}\otimes h_{(2,w)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,w)(1,z)}\otimes\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)R^{(2)}(w)_{(1,z)}h_{(1,w)(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(1,w)(2,z)}\otimes R^{(2)}(w)_{(2,z)}h_{(1,w)(2,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\otimes\left[R^{(1)}(w)\cdot b_{(2,w)}\otimes h_{(2,w)}\right]

H
is a vertex bialgebra

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,w)(1,z)}\otimes\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)\dot{R}^{(2)}(w+z)h_{(1,w)(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(1,w)(2,z)}\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(w)h_{(1,w)(2,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\otimes\left[\dot{R}^{(1)}(z+w)\ddot{R}^{(1)}(w)\cdot b_{(2,w)}\otimes h_{(2,w)}\right]

hexagon identity for
R(w)
, i.e.
(\Delta_{H}(z)\otimes\textup{id})R(w)_{21}\ =\ \dot{R}(z+w)_{31}\ddot{R}(w)_{32}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,w)(1,z)}\otimes\underline{\dot{R}}^{(2)}(z+w)h_{(1,w)(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(1,w)}\cdot b_{(1,w)(2,z)}\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(w)h_{(1,w)(2,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\otimes\left[\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(2,w)}\ddot{R}^{(1)}(w)\cdot b_{(2,w)}\otimes h_{(2,w)}\right]

hexagon identity for
\underline{\dot{R}}(z+w)
, i.e.
(\textup{id}\otimes\Delta_{H}(w))\underline{\dot{R}}(w+z)_{21}\ =\ \underline{R}(z)_{21}\dot{R}(z+w)_{31}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z+w)}\otimes\underline{\dot{R}}^{(2)}(z+w)h_{(1,z+w)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(1,w)}\cdot b_{(2,z+w)(1,w)}\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(w)h_{(2,z+w)(1,w)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\otimes\left[\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(2,w)}\ddot{R}^{(1)}(w)\cdot b_{(2,z+w)(2,w)}\otimes h_{(2,z+w)(2,w)}\right]

coassociativity for
H,B
, i.e.
h_{(1,w)(1,z)}\otimes h_{(1,w)(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2,w)}\ =\ h_{(1,z+w)}\otimes h_{(2,z+w)(1,w)}\otimes h_{(2,z+w)(2,w)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z+w)}\otimes\underline{\dot{R}}^{(2)}(z+w)h_{(1,z+w)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(1,w)}\cdot b_{(2,z+w)(1,w)}\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(w)h_{(2,z+w)(1,w)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\otimes\left[\ddot{R}^{(1)}(w)\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(2,w)}\cdot b_{(2,z+w)(2,w)}\otimes h_{(2,z+w)(2,w)}\right]

commutativity of
H
, i.e.
\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(2,w)}\ddot{R}^{(1)}(w)\ =\ \ddot{R}^{(1)}(w)\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)_{(2,w)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z+w)}\otimes\underline{\dot{R}}^{(2)}(z+w)h_{(1,z+w)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(R^{(1)}(z+w)\cdot b_{(2,z+w)})_{(1,w)}\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(w)(h_{(2,z+w)})_{(1,w)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\otimes\left[\ddot{R}^{(1)}(w)\cdot(\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)\cdot b_{(2,z+w)})_{(2,w)}\otimes(h_{(2,z+w)})_{(2,w)}\right]

H
is a vertex bialgebra

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left(\textup{id}\otimes\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(w)\right)\left((b_{(1,z+w)}\otimes\underline{\dot{R}}^{(2)}(z+w)h_{(1,z+w)})\otimes(\underline{\dot{R}}^{(1)}(z+w)\cdot b_{(2,z+w)}\otimes h_{(2,z+w)})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left(\textup{id}\otimes\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(w)\right)\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b\otimes h,z+w).

Bialgebra axiom

Finally, it remains to show that
B\otimes H
satisfies the bialgebra axiom, i.e.

\Delta^{\textup{ext}}((b\otimes h)\cdot(b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime}),z)=\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b\otimes h,z)\cdot\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime},z).

Again, we compute both sides:

\displaystyle\Delta(b\otimes h\ \cdot\ b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime},z)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \Delta(b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})\otimes h_{(2)}h^{\prime},z)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[(b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime}))_{(1,z)}\otimes R^{(2)}(z)(h_{(2)}h^{\prime})_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[R^{(1)}(z)\cdot(b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime}))_{(2,z)}\otimes(h_{(2)}h^{\prime})_{(2,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[(b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime}))_{(1,z)}\otimes R^{(2)}(z)h_{(2)(1,z)}h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[R^{(1)}(z)\cdot(b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime}))_{(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right]

H
is a vertex bialgebra in
Vect

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}(\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})_{(1,z)})\otimes R^{(2)}(z)h_{(2)(1,z)}h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[R^{(1)}(z)\cdot(\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)b_{(2,z)})(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})_{(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right]

B
is a vertex bialgebra in
(H\text{-}\textup{Mod},\underline{R}(z))

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}(\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1)(1,z)}\cdot b^{\prime}_{(1,z)})\otimes R^{(2)}(z)h_{(2)(1,z)}h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[R^{(1)}(z)\cdot(\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)b_{(2,z)})(h_{(1)(2,z)}\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right]

H
is a vertex bialgebra in
Vect

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}(\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1)(1,z)}\cdot(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\otimes R^{(2)}(z)h_{(2)(1,z)}h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[R^{(1)}(z)\cdot(\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)})(h_{(1)(2,z)}\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}(\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1)(1,z)}\cdot(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\otimes R^{(2)}(z)h_{(2)(1,z)}h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(R^{(1)}(z)_{(1)}\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)})(R^{(1)}(z)_{(2)}h_{(1)(2,z)}\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right]

B
’s product is
H
-linear, i.e.
R(z)\cdot(\dot{b}\ddot{b})\ =\ (R(z)_{(1)}\cdot\dot{b})(R(z)_{(2)}\cdot\ddot{b})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}(\underline{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1)(1,z)}\cdot(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\otimes\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(2)(1,z)}h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\underline{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)})(\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)h_{(1)(2,z)}\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right]

hexagon relation for
R(z)
, i.e.
(\textup{id}\otimes\Delta_{H}(0))R(z)\ =\ \dot{R}_{12}(z)\ddot{R}_{13}(z)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}(\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)_{(1)}h_{(1)(1,z)}\cdot(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\otimes\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)_{(2)}\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(2)(1,z)}h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)})(\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)h_{(1)(2,z)}\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right]

hexagon relation, i.e.
(\Delta_{H}(0)\otimes\textup{id})\dot{R}(z)\ =\ \dot{R}_{13}(z)\underline{R}_{23}(z)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}((\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)_{(1)}h_{(1)(1,z)})\cdot(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\otimes\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)_{(2)}h_{(2)(1,z)}\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot(b_{(2,z)}))(h_{(1)(2,z)}\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\right]

H
commutative

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}((\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)_{(1)}h_{(1,z)(1)})\cdot(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\otimes\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)_{(2)}h_{(1,z)(2)}\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot(b_{(2,z)}))(h_{(2,z)(1)}\cdot\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2,z)(2)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\right]

H
is cocommutative, so
h_{(1)(1,z)}\otimes h_{(2)(1,z)}\otimes h_{(1)(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2)(2,z)}\ =\ h_{(1,z)(1)}\otimes h_{(1,z)(1)}\otimes h_{(2,z)(1)}\otimes h_{(2,z)(2)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[b_{(1,z)}((\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1,z)})_{(1)}\cdot(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\otimes(\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1,z)})_{(2)}\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot(b_{(2,z)}))(h_{(2,z)(1)}\cdot\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2,z)(2)}h^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\right]

H
is a bialgebra

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[(b_{(1,z)}\otimes\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1,z)})\cdot((b^{\prime}_{(1,z)})\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h^{\prime}_{(1,z)})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[(\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot(b_{(2,z)})\otimes h_{(2,z)})\cdot(\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left[(b_{(1,z)}\otimes\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1,z)})\cdot((b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}))\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\left[((\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2,z)}))\cdot(\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2,z)})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \left(b_{(1,z)}\otimes\dot{R}^{(2)}(z)h_{(1,z)}\,\otimes\,\dot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2,z)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\cdot\left(b^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\otimes\ddot{R}^{(2)}(z)h^{\prime}_{(1,z)}\,\otimes\,\ddot{R}^{(1)}(z)\cdot b^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\otimes h^{\prime}_{(2,z)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\ =\ \Delta(b\otimes h,z)\cdot\Delta(b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime},z).

∎

5.3.
Bosonising quiver CoHAs

5.3.1.
Proof of Theorem
5.0.1

We apply the vertex bosonisation Theorem
5.2.2
to

B\ =\ \mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T,\psi},\hskip 42.67912ptH\ =\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}_{T}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}

inside the category
\textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda}
. Note that Theorem
4.5.2
is precisely the statement that
H=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}_{T}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
and
B=\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T,\psi}
satisfy the conditions required to apply vertex bosonisation. It thus remains to compute the bosonised product and vertex coproduct on
\mathcal{A}_{Q,W}^{T,\psi}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}_{T}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}
. Firstly, by equation (
72
) we have that

\displaystyle(b\otimes h)\cdot(b^{\prime}\otimes h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\ =\ b(h_{(1)}\cdot b^{\prime})\otimes h_{(2)}h^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ =\ (b\cdot((\oplus^{*}h)_{(1)}\cup b^{\prime}))\otimes(\oplus^{*}h)_{(2)}\cup h^{\prime}

and from (
69
) we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b\otimes h,z)

\displaystyle\ =\ (b_{(1,z)}\otimes R^{(2)}(z)h_{(1,z)})\otimes(R^{(1)}(z)\cdot b_{(2,z)}\otimes h_{(2,z)})

\displaystyle\ =\ (\sigma R(z))_{23}\cup\left((e^{zT\otimes\textup{id}}\oplus^{*}h)_{13}\otimes\Delta(b,z)_{24}\right).

Note that in the symmetric setting
R(z)=R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
.
Thus vertex bosonisation Theorem
5.2.2
implies that this forms a nonlocal vertex bialgebra inside
\textup{Vect}^{T}_{\Lambda}
.

5.3.2.
Explicit formula

The bosonised coproduct is

\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(b\otimes h,z)\ =\ \sigma(\frac{c(\sigma^{*}\textup{Ext}^{\vee},z^{-1})}{c(\textup{Ext},z^{-1})})_{23}\Psi(\textup{Ext},-z)_{13}\cdot((\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}b)_{13}\otimes(\textup{act}_{z,1}^{*}\oplus^{*}h)_{24})

from the definition of
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
and the vertex coproducts on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{Q,W}
and
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
. We will continue exploring explicit forms for this coproduct in the following section.

5.3.3.
Non-symmetric case

When
Q
is nonsymmetric,
R(z)
no longer equals
R_{\textup{taut}}(z)
. Therefore, the latter does not satisfy the spectral hexagon relation (as
\tilde{\chi}\neq 0
in Lemma
4.4.4
) which is needed when proving coassociativity of the bosonised
\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(z)
, although the proof of the bialgebra property carries through. On the other hand, the former is no longer valued in the tautological ring
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
.

We expect the fix to be redefining the tautological ring as generated by the coefficients of the full Joyce
R
-matrix.

6.
Obtaining Drinfeld’s coproduct on the Yangian

For a Dynkin quiver
Q
, we have an associative algebra called the Yangian
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})
with a compatible
Drinfeld
vertex coproduct
\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)
as discussed in section
0.4
.
In this case, Yang and Zhao
[
54
]
