identified the Yangian with the double of the cohomological Hall algebra of the tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
, an isomorphism which restricts to isomorphisms on the positive and Borel parts:

(74)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})^{\geq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}^{T,\psi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The main result (Theorem
6.2.7
) of this section is that the identification (
74
) exchanges the Drinfeld and extended Joyce–Liu vertex coproducts:

\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)\ =\ \Delta^{\textup{ext}}(z),

where the left and right sides are defined in section
6.2.6
and in Theorem
2.3.1
. An analogue of this theorem for a localised type coproduct was proven in
[
54
]
.

In order to prove this we have to compute the bosonised Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct explicitly on spherical elements for tripled quivers, which we do in Proposition
6.1.9
. To this general case
[
5
,
50
]
extended the identification (
74
) for arbitrary tripled quivers
\widetilde{Q}
using the MO Yangian. Therefore, these computations also give a computation on spherical elements of Drinfeld type coproducts on positive halves of MO Yangians.

6.1.
CoHA of tripled quiver with canonical cubic potential

6.1.1.

We now compute the Joyce–Liu coproduct on spherical elements in the case that the quiver is a triple
\widetilde{Q}
with its canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{W}
, as defined in section
1.1
, and the rank one weight function acting on edges by

\textup{wt}(e)\ =\ 1,\hskip 14.22636pt\textup{wt}(e^{*})\ =\ -1,\hskip 14.22636pt\textup{wt}(\omega_{i})\ =\ -2

and
T
be the associated rank one torus with
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)\simeq\textbf{{C}}[\hbar/2]
. Note that this quiver is of course symmetric. Furthermore, we have

(75)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,\delta_{i}}^{T},\varphi_{W})\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}_{T}(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m})[-2]

since
\widetilde{W}|_{\delta_{i}}=0
.

We will consider the twisted CoHA
\mathcal{A}^{T_{\hbar},\chi}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
with respect to the twist
\psi(d_{1},d_{2})=\chi_{\widetilde{Q}}(d_{1},d_{2})
, which satisfies
\psi(d_{1},d_{2})+\psi(d_{2},d_{1})=\tau_{\widetilde{Q}}(d_{1},d_{2})
; see section
4.4.16
for more on sign twists. For tripled quivers the Künneth Assumption (
31
) is known. Note that in general for quiver without loops, outside the ADE case, the CoHA of a tripled quiver is
not
spherically generated. See
[
11
, Prop. 5.7]

6.1.2.
Defining spherical elements

Let

R_{i}(z)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}[x]((z^{-1}))

denote the image of the swapped
R
-matrix
\sigma(R_{\textup{taut}}(z))
under the map

\displaystyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}((z^{-1}))

\displaystyle\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,\delta_{i}}^{T})((z^{-1}))

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}[x]((z^{-1}))

where we identify
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,\delta_{i}})\simeq\textbf{{C}}[x]
for
x=1\otimes x_{i,1}
the first chern class of the line bundle
\mathcal{E}_{i,\delta_{i}}
.

Lemma 6.1.3
.

For any
i\in Q_{0}
,
R_{i}(z)
is an expansion of a power series

\ \Phi_{i}(x-z)\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\left[\left[(x-z)^{-1}\right]\right],

where writing
u=x-z
, the component of
\Phi_{i}(u)
in the
d
th dimension vector
9
9

9

i.e., its image under the map
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}[[u^{-1}]]\to\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})[[u^{-1}]]
.
is

\displaystyle\Phi_{i,d}(u)=

(76)

\displaystyle\bigsqcap_{1\leq n\leq d_{i}}\left(\frac{u-x_{i,n}+\hbar}{u-x_{i,n}-\hbar}\right)\cdot\bigsqcap_{e\in Q_{1}}\frac{\bigsqcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}s(e)=i\\
1\leq n\leq d_{t(e)}\end{subarray}}(u-x_{t(e),n}-\hbar/2)\bigsqcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}t(e)=i\\
1\leq n\leq d_{s(e)}\end{subarray}}(u-x_{s(e),n}-\hbar/2)}{\bigsqcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}s(e)=i\\
1\leq n\leq d_{t(e)}\end{subarray}}(u-x_{t(e),n}+\hbar/2)\bigsqcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}t(e)=i\\
1\leq n\leq d_{s(e)}\end{subarray}}(u-x_{s(e),n}+\hbar/2)}

as an element of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,d})[[u^{-1}]]
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.4.15
. Spelling this out, we compute
\sigma(R_{\delta_{i},d}(z))
using the formulas in Proposition
1.4.3
and grouping the edges of the tripled quiver as
\widetilde{Q}_{1}=Q_{1}\sqcup Q^{\textup{op}}_{1}\sqcup Q_{0}
, applying
\textup{act}^{*}_{1}
and setting
u=x-z
with
x=1\otimes x_{i,1}
.
∎

We call the coefficients of the power series

\Phi_{i}(u)\ =\ 1+\hbar\sum_{r\geq 0}\Phi_{i,r}u^{-r-1}\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}\left[\left[u^{-1}\right]\right]

the
tautological spherical elements
. We define the
spherical CoHA elements
as the coefficients
x^{(r)}_{i,1}
:

x_{i}(u)\ =\ \sum_{r\geq 0}x^{(r)}_{i,1}u^{-r-1}\ \in\ \mathcal{A}^{T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}[[u^{-1}]],

defined as the elements corresponding to
x^{r}\in\textbf{{C}}[x]\simeq\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m})
under the identification (
75
). We stress the notational difference between
x_{i,1}^{r}
, an element in the cohomology ring, and
x_{i,1}^{(r)}
an element of critical cohomology.

6.1.4.
Remarks about relations to work of Yang-Zhao

We note that the formula in equation (
76
) matches up with the formula in
[
54
, Section 1.3]
. Therefore, we believe that our extended vertex coproduct is closely related to the coproducts defined in
[
54
, Section 2]
and
[
45
, Section 2.1]
.

6.1.5.

In Lemma
4.3.2
, we produced a coproduct on the tautological ring. It would also be convenient to write this coproduct for the elements
\Phi_{i,r}
.

Lemma 6.1.6
.

The coproduct and coaction

\oplus^{*}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}},\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}[z]

defined in Lemma
4.3.2
send respectively

(77)

\Phi_{i}(u)\ \mapsto\ \Phi_{i}(u)\otimes\Phi_{i}(u),\hskip 42.67912pt\Phi_{i}(u)\ \mapsto\ \Phi_{i}(u-z)

More precisely, we have under the direct sum pullback

\Phi_{i,0}\ \mapsto\ 1\otimes\Phi_{i,0}+\Phi_{i,0}\otimes 1

and

\Phi_{i,r}\ \mapsto\ 1\otimes\Phi_{i,r}+\Phi_{i,r}\otimes 1+\hbar\left(\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r-1}\Phi_{i,r_{1}}\otimes\Phi_{i,r_{2}}\right)

when
r\geq 1
, and under the action map pullback

\Phi_{i,r}\ \mapsto\ \sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=r}\binom{r}{r_{1}}\Phi_{i,r_{1}}z^{r_{2}}.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
4.4.4
, in particular from equation
51
and
4.4.3
that

(\oplus^{*}\,\otimes\,\textup{id})\sigma(R_{\textup{taut}}(z))\ =\ \sigma(R_{\textup{taut},13}(z))\sigma(R_{\textup{taut},23}(z)),\hskip 14.22636pt\textup{act}_{w,1}^{*}\sigma(R_{\textup{taut}}(z))\ =\ \sigma(R_{\textup{taut}}(z+w)).

Taking the image of this equation under the map
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\to\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,\delta_{i}})
on the third tensor factor gives (
77
) by Lemma
6.1.3
.
∎

These elements moreover give a new generating set for the tautological ring:

Proposition 6.1.7
.

There is an algebra isomorphism

(78)

\displaystyle\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)[\xi_{i,r}\ :\ i\in Q_{0},\,r\geq 0]

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}

\displaystyle\xi_{i,r}

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \Phi_{i,r}.

Proof.

Since both vector spaces have the same graded dimensions, it is enough to show that the map is injective, i.e. that the
\Phi_{i,r}
are algebraically independent.

Since both sides of (
78
) are free
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}T)
-modules, it is enough to show that the
\Phi_{i,r}
are algebraically independent after setting
\hbar=0
. Note that this follows from

(79)

\Phi_{i,d}(u)\ =\ 1+\hbar\sum_{r\geq 0}r!\left(2\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i,d})-\sum_{e\,:\,i\to j}\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{j,d})-\sum_{e\,:\,j\to i}\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{j,d})\right)u^{-r-1}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})

since the bracketed expressions for fixed
r
form a basis for
\textbf{{C}}\{\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})\ :\ i\in Q_{0}\}
by nondegeneracy of the Cartan matrix of
Q
, hence varying over all
i,r
they inherit the property of being algebraically independent from the
\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
, which are algebraically independent by the proof of Proposition
4.1.3
.

To prove (
79
), we use
f^{\prime}(u)=f(u)(\log f(u))^{\prime}
to compute that the first order
\hbar
term is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi_{i,d}(u)}{\partial\hbar}|_{\hbar=0}

\displaystyle\ =\ \Phi_{i}(u)\frac{\partial\log(\Phi_{i,d}(u))}{\partial\hbar}|_{\hbar=0}

\displaystyle\ =\ \Phi_{i,d}(u)\left(\sum_{n=1}^{d_{i}}\frac{\partial_{\hbar}V_{n}(u)}{V_{n}(u)}+\sum_{e\,:\,i\to j}\sum_{n=1}^{d_{j}}\frac{\partial_{\hbar}E_{n}^{e}(u)}{E_{n}^{e}(u)}+\sum_{e\,:\,j\to i}\sum_{n=1}^{d_{j}}\frac{\partial_{\hbar}E_{n}^{e}(u)}{E_{n}^{e}(u)}\right)|_{\hbar=0}

\displaystyle\ =\ \sum_{n=1}^{d_{i}}\frac{\partial V_{n}(u)}{\partial\hbar}+\sum_{e\,:\,i\to j}\sum_{n=1}^{d_{j}}\frac{\partial E_{n}^{e}(u)}{\partial\hbar}+\sum_{e\,:\,j\to i}\sum_{n=1}^{d_{j}}\frac{\partial E_{n}^{e}(u)}{\partial\hbar}|_{\hbar=0}

where
V_{n}(u),E^{e}_{n}(u)
are the factors of
\Phi_{i,d}(u)
in the expression (
76
) labelled by a specific edge of
\widetilde{Q}
and
n
th chern root, and in the third equality we used that they all are equal to
1
when
\hbar=0
.

The result then follows upon noting that if
f(u)=\frac{u-x+\hbar}{u-x-\hbar}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f(u)}{\partial\hbar}|_{\hbar=0}

\displaystyle\ =\ 2\frac{1}{u-x}\ =\ 2\sum_{r\geq 0}x^{r}u^{-r-1},

and that
r!\textup{ch}_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{j})
is a sum over
r
th powers of all the chern roots of
\mathcal{E}_{j}
.

∎

6.1.8.
Remark

From the point of view of the above Lemma another definition of the Cartan part could be given by taking coefficients of the
R
-matrix. This approach could potentially generalise to study Joyce coproducts outside quiver settings.

In the remainder of this subsection we compute the extended Joyce–Liu vertex coproduct on the extended CoHA of the tripled quiver with canonical cubic potential.

Proposition 6.1.9
.

Let
\widetilde{Q}
be the triple of any quiver
Q
. Then the extended Joyce-Liu vertex coproduct on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
acts as

(80)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(\Phi_{i}(u))

\displaystyle\ =\ \Phi_{i}(u-z)\otimes\Phi_{i}(u)

(81)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(x^{(n)}_{i,1},z)

\displaystyle\ =\ (\tau_{z}(x^{(n)}_{i,1})\otimes 1+1\otimes x^{(n)}_{i,1})

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ +\ \hbar\sum_{N\geq 0}\left(\sum_{p=0}^{N}(-1)^{p+1}\binom{N}{p}\Phi_{i,p}\otimes x^{(n+N-p)}_{i,1}\right)z^{-N-1}

Proof.

We unwind the definition of the extended coproduct (
67
) from Theorem
5.0.1
. We will use again and again the fact that the components of the
R
-matrix in components
0
- and
\delta_{i}
-components of
\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q}
are the cohomology classes

(82)

R(z)_{(d,0)}\ =\ 1\otimes 1,\hskip 28.45274ptR(z)_{(0,d)}\ =\ 1\otimes 1,\hskip 28.45274pt\sigma R(z)_{(\delta_{i},d)}\ =\ \Phi_{i,d}(1\otimes x_{i,1}-z),

since the restrictions of
Ext
to the the
(0,d)
and
(d,0)
connected components are zero and the third equality is the definition of
\Phi_{i}(z)
.

Writing as a vector space
\mathcal{A}^{T,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}=\mathcal{A}^{T}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{T})_{\textup{taut}}
, equation (
67
) first gives that

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(1\otimes\Phi_{i}(u))

\displaystyle\ =\ (\sigma R(z))_{23}\cdot(1\otimes\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\Phi_{i}(u)_{(1)})\otimes(1\otimes\Phi_{i}(u)_{(2)})

\displaystyle\ =\ (1\otimes\textup{act}_{z}^{*}\Phi_{i}(u))\otimes(1\otimes\Phi_{i}(u))

\displaystyle\ =\ (1\otimes\Phi_{i}(u-z))\otimes(1\otimes\Phi_{i}(u))

since the
R
-matrix acts trivially by (
82
) and by the formula in Lemma
6.1.6
for the coproduct and coaction on
\Phi_{i}(u)
.

Before showing (
81
) and finishing the proof, we compute the unextended Joyce–Liu coproduct:

\displaystyle\Delta(x^{(n)}_{i,1},z)

\displaystyle\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext},z)\cdot\textup{act}^{*}_{z,1}(\oplus^{*}x^{(n)}_{i,1})

\displaystyle\ =\ \Psi(\textup{Ext},z)\cdot\left(\textup{act}^{*}_{z}x^{(n)}_{i,1}\otimes 1+1\otimes x^{(n)}_{i,1}\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ \tau_{z}x^{(n)}_{i,1}\otimes 1+1\otimes x^{(n)}_{i,1}

where the second equality is true because the direct sum map in this case is

\oplus_{\delta_{i},0}\sqcup\oplus_{0,\delta_{i}}\ :\ (\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,\delta_{i}}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,0})\ \sqcup\ (\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,0}\times_{\textup{B}T}\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,\delta_{i}})\ \to\ \mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,\delta_{i}}

is after identifying
\mathcal{M}_{Q,0}\simeq\textup{pt}
the identity map on each component, and the third equality is true because
\textup{Ext}_{(0,d)}=\textup{Ext}_{(d,0)}
is zero.

As a second preparation, we compute the restriction of the
R
-matrix using power series expansions:

\displaystyle\sigma R(z)_{(\delta_{i},-)}

\displaystyle\ =\ \Phi_{i}(1\otimes x_{i,1}-z)

\displaystyle\ =\ 1+\hbar\sum_{r\geq 0}\Phi_{i,r}(1\otimes x_{i,1}-z)^{-r-1}

\displaystyle\ =\ 1+\hbar\sum_{r\geq 0}\Phi_{i,r}\sum_{k\geq 0}(-1)^{r+1}\binom{r+k}{r}(1\otimes x_{i,1})^{k}z^{-(k+r+1)}

\displaystyle\ =\ 1+\hbar\sum_{N\geq 0}\left(\sum_{p\geq 0}(-1)^{-(p+1)}\binom{N}{p}\Phi_{i,p}\otimes x^{N-p}_{i,1}\right)z^{-(N+1)}

as elements of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q})_{\textup{taut}}\otimes_{T}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}^{T}_{Q,\delta_{i}})[[z^{-1}]]
, i.e.

(83)

R^{(2)}(z)\otimes R^{(1)}(z)_{\delta_{i}}\ =\ 1\otimes 1+\hbar\left(\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{p\geq 0}(-1)^{-(p+1)}\binom{N}{p}\Phi_{i,p}\otimes x^{N-p}_{i,1}\right)z^{-(N+1)}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(x^{(n)}_{i,1}\otimes 1,z)

\displaystyle\ =\ \left((1\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1)+\hbar\left(\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{p\geq 0}(-1)^{-(p+1)}\binom{N}{p}(1\otimes\Phi_{i,p})\otimes(x_{i,1}^{(N-p)}\otimes 1)\right)z^{-(N+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\cdot\left((\tau_{z}x^{(n)}_{i,1}\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1)+(1\otimes 1)\otimes(x^{(n)}_{i,1}\otimes 1)\right)

\displaystyle\ =\ \left((\tau_{z}x^{(n)}_{i,1}\otimes 1)\otimes(1\otimes 1)+(1\otimes 1)\otimes(x^{(n)}_{i,1}\otimes 1)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ +\ \hbar\left(\sum_{N\geq 0}\sum_{p\geq 0}(-1)^{-(p+1)}\binom{N}{p}(1\otimes\Phi_{i,p})\otimes(x^{(n+N-p)}_{i,1}\otimes 1)\otimes\right)z^{-(N+1)}\cdot

where in the last line we used that by definition of the spherical elements that

x^{k}_{i,1}\cdot x^{(n)}_{i,1}\ =\ x^{(n+k)}_{i,1}.

This proves (
81
) and finishes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Comparison to Drinfeld’s coproduct

6.2.1.

We now compare our constructions in the ADE quiver case to ADE Yangians. We start with the generators and relations definition due to Drinfeld
[
16
]
.

6.2.2.
ADE Yangians

Let
Q
be any ADE quiver. Let
c_{ij}=2\delta_{ij}-a_{ij}-a_{ji}
is symmetrised Cartan matrix of the quiver
Q
, where
a_{ij}
are the number of arrows from vertex
i
to vertex
j
.

Definition 6.2.3
.

The Yangian
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
is the
\textbf{{C}}[\hbar]
linear algebra generated by elements
\{x^{\pm}_{i,r},\xi_{i,r}\}_{i\in Q_{0},r\in\textbf{{N}}}
, satisfying the following relations for every
i,j\in Q_{0}
and
r,s\in\textbf{{N}}
:

(R1)

[\xi_{i,r},\xi_{j,s}]=0

(R2)

[\xi_{i,0},x_{j,s}^{\pm}]=\pm c_{ij}x_{j,s}^{\pm}

(R3)

[\xi_{i,r+1},x^{\pm}_{j,s}]-[\xi_{i,r},x^{\pm}_{j,s+1}]=\pm\hbar\frac{c_{ij}}{2}(\xi_{i,r}x^{\pm}_{j,s}+x^{\pm}_{j,s}\xi_{i,r})

(R4)

[x^{\pm}_{i,r+1},x^{\pm}_{j,s}]-[x^{\pm}_{i,r},x^{\pm}_{j,s+1}]=\pm\hbar\frac{c_{ij}}{2}(x^{\pm}_{i,r}x^{\pm}_{j,s}+x^{\pm}_{j,s}x^{\pm}_{i,r})

(R5)

[x^{+}_{i,r},x^{-}_{j,s}]=\delta_{ij}\xi_{i,r+s}

(R6)

Assume
i\neq j
and set
m=1-c_{ij}
. For any

r_{1},\cdots,r_{m}\in\textbf{{N}}
and
s\in\textbf{{N}}
we have

\sum_{\pi\in\textup{Sym}_{m}}\left[x^{\pm}_{i,r_{\pi(1)}},\left[x^{\pm}_{i,r_{\pi(2)}},\left[\cdots,\left[x^{\pm}_{i,r_{\pi(m)}},x^{\pm}_{j,s}\right]\cdots\right]\right]\right.=0

Analogously to subsection
6.1.2
we define the generating series

\xi_{i}(u)\ =\ 1+\hbar\sum_{r\geq 0}\xi_{i,r}u^{-r-1}\ \in\ Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})[[u^{-1}]],\hskip 42.67912ptx^{\pm}_{i}(u)\ =\ \sum_{r\geq 0}x^{\pm}_{i,r}u^{-r-1}\ \in\ Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})[[u^{-1}]].

6.2.4.
Comparison with the CoHA as an algebra

We will consider the Borel part of the Yangian, Let
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{\geq 0}\subseteq Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
be the subalgebra generated by
\{x^{+}_{i,1},\xi_{i,r}\}_{i\in Q_{0},r\in\textbf{{N}}}
. Then we have the following theorem, which is essentially
[
55
, Thm. D]
in our formulation.

Proposition 6.2.5
.

The morphism

\displaystyle f:Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\ \to\ \mathcal{A}^{T,\chi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

\displaystyle x^{+}_{i,r}

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ x^{(r)}_{i,1}

\displaystyle\xi_{i,r}

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\ \Phi_{i,r}

is an isomorphism of algebras.

Proof.

It is already proven in
[
54
]
that this morphism when restricted to
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})^{>0}
is an isomorphism and we have proved in Proposition
6.1.7
that the morphism restricted to Cartan
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})^{0}
is an isomorphism. Thus it suffices to show that this morphism
f
is well defined: we have to check relations (R2) and (R3), since all other relations are implied by
f
being well-defined on
>0
and
0
parts. We will write
1\otimes h
when view an element
h
of the tautological ring as an element of the extended algebra, and similarly
b\otimes 1
for
b
in the CoHA.

To show (R2), we first note

f([\xi_{i,0},x^{+}_{j,s}])=[1\otimes\Phi_{i,0},x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1].

Thus by definition of the product
\star
of (
68
), we have

\displaystyle(1\otimes\Phi_{i,0})\star(x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1)

\displaystyle\ =\ (\Phi_{i,0})_{(1)}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes(\Phi_{i,0})_{(2)}

\displaystyle\ =\ x^{(s)}_{i,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,0}+\Phi_{i,0}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1

\displaystyle\ =\ x^{(s)}_{i,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,0}+c_{ij}(x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1)

where we computed the coproduct of
\Phi_{i,0}
in Lemma
6.1.6
. Furthermore, by definition
\Phi_{i,0}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}=\Phi_{i,0,\delta_{j}}\cup x^{(s)}_{j,1}
and so by equation (
79
), it follows that in fact
\Phi_{i,0,\delta_{j}}=c_{ij}
. Similarly, by definition of
\star
we have

(x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1)\star(1\otimes\Phi_{i,0})\ =\ x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,0}

and therefore

[1\otimes\Phi_{i,0},x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1]\ =\ c_{ij}x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1\ =\ f(c_{ij}x^{+}_{i,s})

and thus the relation (R2) is satisfied.

We now check (R3). We have

[1\otimes\Phi_{i,r+1},x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1]\ =\ \Phi_{i,r+1}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1+\hbar\left(\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=r}\Phi_{i,k_{1}}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,k_{2}}\right)

using same strategy as above, since

(1\otimes\Phi_{i,r+1})\star(x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1)=x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,r+1}+\Phi_{i,r+1}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1+\hbar\left(\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=r}\Phi_{i,k_{1}}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,k_{2}}\right),

therefore combining this with
(x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1)*(1\otimes\Phi_{i,r+1})=x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,r+1}
gives us that

[1\otimes\Phi_{i,r},x^{(s+1)}_{j,1}\otimes 1]\ =\ \Phi_{i,r}\cdot x^{(s+1)}_{j,1}\otimes 1+\hbar\left(\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=r-1}\Phi_{i,k_{1}}\cdot x^{(s+1)}_{j,1}\otimes\Phi_{i,k_{2}}\right)

and so it remains to compute the coefficients of these series. Note that by definition
\Phi_{i,r}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}=\Phi_{i,r,\delta_{j}}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}
. If there is no arrow from
i
to
j
in
\widetilde{Q}
,
\Phi_{i,r,\delta_{j}}=0
and hence the commutator satisfies:

[1\otimes\Phi_{i,r+1},x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1]-[1\otimes\Phi_{i,r},x^{(s+1)}_{j,1}\otimes 1]\ =\ 0

which is exactly the relation (R3) is satisfied. If not, then

\Phi_{i,\delta_{j}}(u)=\delta_{i,j}\frac{u-x_{j,1}+\hbar}{u-x_{j,1}-\hbar}+\delta_{i\to j}\frac{u-x_{j,1}-\hbar/2}{u-x_{j,1}+\hbar/2}+\delta_{j\to i}\frac{u-x_{j,1}-\hbar/2}{u-x_{j,1}+\hbar/2}

where
\delta_{i\to j}
is 1 if there is a arrow from
i
to
j
in the quiver
Q
and
0
otherwise. This gives that

\Phi_{i,r,\delta_{j}}=2\delta_{ij}(x_{j,1}+\hbar)^{r}-\delta_{i\to j}(x_{j,1}-\hbar/2)^{r}-\delta_{j\to i}(x_{j,1}-\hbar/2)^{r}

and thus

\displaystyle\Phi_{i,r+1}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}

\displaystyle\ =\ 2\delta_{ij}(x_{j,1}+\hbar)^{r+1}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}-\delta_{i\to j}(x_{j,1}-\hbar/2)^{r+1}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}-\delta_{j\to i}(x_{j,1}-\hbar/2)^{r+1}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}

\displaystyle\ =\ \Phi_{i,r}\cdot x^{(s+1)}_{j,1}-\hbar/2(2\delta_{ij}-\delta_{i\to j}-\delta_{j\to i})\Phi_{i,r}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}=\Phi_{i,r}\cdot x^{(s+1)}_{j,1}-\hbar/2(c_{ij})\Phi_{i,r}\cdot x^{(s)}_{j,1}.

Thus it follows that

\displaystyle[1\otimes\Phi_{i,r+1},x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1]-[1\otimes\Phi_{i,r},x^{(s+1)}_{j,1}\otimes 1]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\ =\ c_{ij}\hbar/2\left(1\otimes\Phi_{i,r})*(x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1)+(x^{(s)}_{j,1}\otimes 1)*(1\otimes\Phi_{i,r})\right)

and so (R3) follows.
∎

The cohomological Hall algebra on the right has a coproduct, as we defined in previous sections. The Yangian also a has coproduct, due to Drinfeld which we now recall.

6.2.6.
Drinfeld’s coproduct

We now define the vertex coproduct due to Drinfeld
[
16
]
and Gautam–Toledano-Laredo
[
22
]
, who understood how to express it as a Laurent power series in
z^{-1}
. First let
\tau_{z}:Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})\to Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})[z]
be the map defined by its action on generating series:

x^{\pm}_{i}(u)\mapsto x_{i}^{\pm}(u-z)\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{i}(u)\mapsto\xi_{i}(u-z),

then by
[
22
, Prop. 3.3, Thm. 3.4]
: the following so-called
deformed Drinfeld coproduct
defines an algebra morphism

(84)

\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)\ :\ Y_{\hbar}^{\geq 0}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})\to Y_{\hbar}^{\geq 0}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})\otimes_{\hbar}Y_{\hbar}^{\geq 0}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})((z^{-1})).

if we set

\displaystyle\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(\xi_{i}(u),z)

\displaystyle\ =\ \xi_{i}(u-z)\otimes\xi_{i}(z)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(x_{i,n}^{+},z)

\displaystyle\ =\ \tau_{z}(x_{i,n}^{+})\otimes 1\ +\ 1\otimes x_{i,n}^{+}\ +\ \hbar\sum_{N\geq 0}\left(\sum_{p=0}^{N}(-1)^{p+1}\binom{N}{p}\xi_{i,p}\otimes x_{i,n+N-p}\right)z^{-N-1}.

Our main Theorem in this section is then:

Theorem 6.2.7
.

[Drinfeld and Joyce–Liu coproducts agree]
Let Q be any ADE quiver. Then the map
f
of Proposition
6.2.5

\displaystyle f:Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ \mathcal{A}^{T,\chi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}

is an isomorphism of vertex bialgebras, intertwining the meromorphic Drinfeld coproduct on
Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})^{\geq 0}
with the extended Joyce-Liu coproduct on
\mathcal{A}^{T,\chi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}
. More precisely,
f
is a map of algebras and the following diagram commutes

(85)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})^{\geq 0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})^{\geq 0}\otimes_{\hbar}Y_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q})^{\geq 0}((z^{-1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{T,\chi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{T,\chi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}\otimes_{T}\mathcal{A}^{T,\chi,\textup{ext}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{W}}((z^{-1}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\textup{Dr}}(z)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\textup{ext}}(z)}

Proof.

The map
f
is an algebra isomorphism by Proposition
6.2.5
. By Proposition
6.1.9
, the formulas for the Drinfeld and extended Joyce–Liu coproducts match up via the morphism
f
.
∎

7.
Vertex bialgebras for symmetric quivers with no potential

In this section we will consider our constructions in the special case when
W=0
and the quiver is symmetric. In this case vertex algebra structures on the Cohomological Hall algebra were already considered in
[
33
]
and
[
15
]
. Our goal is to give another characterization of the vertex algebra structure in this case and compare our constructions to
[
15
]
. Along the way, we give another proof of cohomological integrality for symmetric quivers with no potential.

7.0.1.
Warning about sign twists

We make a break from the conventions in the rest of the paper here by working in the
\tau
-twisted symmetric monoidal category
\textup{Vect}_{\mathbf{Z}\times\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}}
and working with the untwisted CoHA
\mathcal{A}_{Q}
of a symmetric quiver. Then by Theorem
4.5.2
the Joyce coproduct is colocal up to the swap morphism

\beta^{\tau}:v\otimes w\mapsto(-1)^{|v||w|+\tau}w\otimes v.

for elements
v,w\in\mathcal{A}_{Q}
. Suppose
v\in\mathcal{A}_{Q,d}
and
w\in\mathcal{A}_{Q,e}
for some dimension vectors
d,e
. Then since
\mathcal{A}_{Q,d}
is defined to be
\textup{H}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d},\mathbf{Q}[-\chi(d,d)])
, if
v\in\mathcal{A}_{Q,d}
then
(-1)^{|v|}=(-1)^{\chi(d,d)}
as the cohomology
\textup{H}^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{Q,d})
is concentrated in even cohomological degrees. Since
\tau(d,e)=\chi(d,d)\chi(e,e)+\chi(d,e)
,
(-1)^{\tau+|v||w|}=(-1)^{\chi(d,e)}
. Thus in this case,
\beta^{\tau}
is same as

\beta^{\chi}_{0}:v\otimes w\mapsto(-1)^{\chi}w\otimes v.

Therefore, we obtain an honest colocal vertex coalgebra in the symmetric monoidal structure arising from
\beta^{\chi}_{0}
. This is the same as the convention used in
[
15
]
(See Remark 4.2).

In this section we will consider the
\mathbf{N}^{{Q}_{0}}\times\mathbf{Z}
-graded dual
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}
of
\mathcal{A}_{Q}
and use the following theorem

Theorem 7.0.2
.

[
27
, Thm. 6.1]

There is a functor, which given a colocal coassociative vertex coalgebra produces a vertex algebra.

(86)

\displaystyle F:\textup{CoVertex}_{\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle\to\textup{Vertex}_{\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbf{Z}}

(87)

\displaystyle V=\bigoplus_{(d,k)\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbf{Z}}V_{d,k}

\displaystyle\mapsto F(V)=\bigoplus_{(d,k)\in\mathbf{N}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbf{Z}}V^{\vee}_{d,-k}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q,0}
now has a vertex algebra and a cocommutative coalgebra structure. By the
0
-potential version of Theorem
4.5.2

\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}
is a cocommutative coalgebra coming from the dual of the commutative CoHA product and is a vertex algebra coming from the dual of the vertex coproduct.

7.1.
Structural results for cocommutative vertex bialgebras

Cocommutative vertex bialgebras have a similar structure theory to cocommutative bialgebras. We start by defining the analogue of a universal enveloping algebra.
We define the
Universal chiral envelope
in the following way.
Let
L
be a vertex Lie algebra as in
[
26
, Definition 3.6]
. Then define
\mathfrak{g}_{L}
to be the Lie algebra

(88)

L\otimes\mathbf{C}[t,t^{-1}]/\textup{im}\partial

where
\partial
is the translation operator

(89)

D\otimes\textup{id}+\textup{id}\otimes\frac{d}{dt}

on
L\otimes\mathbf{C}[t,t^{-1}]
induced by the translation operator
D
on
L
.
We have a decomposition

(90)

\mathfrak{g}_{L}=\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{L}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{L}

where
\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{L}=\textup{span}\{a\otimes t^{i}\mid i<0,\,a\in L\}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{L}=\textup{span}\{a\otimes t^{i}\mid i\geq 0,\,a\in L\}
.

Take the trivial
\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{L}
module structure on
\mathbf{C}
and define

(91)

\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(L)=\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{g}_{L}\otimes_{\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{L}}\otimes\mathbf{C}

then
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(L)
is a vertex algebra and we have an isomorphism
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(L)\simeq\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{L}
of
\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{L}
- modules.
We now summarise some structural results about cocommutative vertex bialgebras from
[
26
]
.

Theorem 7.1.1

( Structure of cocommutative vertex bialgebras )
.

(1)

Let
V
be a vertex algebra with a compatible cocommutative coproduct
\Delta
. Denote by
P(V)
the set of primitive elements with respect to
\Delta
, then
P(V)
has a vertex Lie algebra structure induced from
V
and
P(V)
is a vertex Lie subalgebra of
V
.
[
26
, Prop. 4.8]

(2)

(Milnor-Moore) Let
V
be a connected cocommutative vertex bialgebra, then we have an isomorphism of vertex algebras
[
26
, Thm. 4.13]

(92)

V\simeq\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P(V)).

and an isomorphism of coalgebras
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P(V))\simeq\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{P(V)}
.

(3)

(PBW theorem) We have the isomorphism of vector spaces

(93)

\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P(V))\simeq\textup{Sym}(\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{P(V)}).

7.2.
Cohomological integrality via vertex algebras

Note that since
\mathcal{A}_{Q}
is connected, we can now use the Theorem recalled above to obtain cohomological integrality and also compute the algebra structure of
\mathcal{A}_{Q}
.

We start by recalling two different constructions of symmetric algebras.
Let
V
be a graded vector space with finite dimensional pieces. There is a natural action of
S_{n}
on each
V^{\otimes n}
, this then induces an action of all the symmetric groups on the tensor algebra
T(V)
. Then define

(1)

the
symmetric algebra

\textup{Sym}(V)
as the bialgebra with product induced by the quotient map
T(V)\to\textup{Sym}(V)
and coproduct
\Delta
induced by
\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
.

(2)

the
invariant algebra

\Gamma(V)\xhookrightarrow{}T(V)
as the subspace of invariant tensors.
\Gamma(V)
is an algebra with the shuffle algebra product

(94)

(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})*(w_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes w_{m})\mapsto\frac{1}{n!m!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n+m}}\sigma(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}\otimes w_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes w_{m}).

Lemma 7.2.1
.

Let
V
be a graded vector space with finite dimensional graded pieces. Then we have the following isomorphisms

(1)

We have an isomorphism of algebras
(\textup{Sym}V)^{\vee}\simeq\Gamma(V^{*})
, where the algebra structure on
(\textup{Sym}V)^{\vee}
is induced by the coproduct
\Delta
on
\textup{Sym}(V)

(2)

We have an isomorphism of algebras
\textup{Sym}(V)\simeq\Gamma(V)
.

Proof.

Part
(1)
is
[
39
, Exercise 1.8.7]
.
For part
(2)
we can consider the map

\displaystyle\textup{Sym}(V)

\displaystyle\to\Gamma(V)

(95)

\displaystyle v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sigma(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})

which is an isomorphism. This is clearly a morphism of algebras since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!m!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n+m}}\sigma\left(\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in S_{n},\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in S_{m}}\sigma^{\prime}(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})\sigma^{\prime\prime}(w_{1}\otimes\cdots w_{m})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n+m}}\sigma(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}\otimes w_{1}\cdots\otimes w_{m})

because we are repeating
n!m!
permutations in the sum on LHS.

Let
f\in\Gamma^{n}(V)
, where
\Gamma^{n}(V)\subset\Gamma(V)
is subspace of invariant tensors in
V^{\otimes n}
. We define its inverse as
1/n!\overline{f}
where
\overline{f}
is the image of the composition
\Gamma(V)\subset T(V)\to\textup{Sym}(V)
. We linearly extend this to morphism to define the inverse
\Gamma(V)\to\textup{Sym}(V)
. Clearly the composition is identity since
\sigma(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})=v_{1}\otimes\cdots v_{n}
in the quotient
\textup{Sym}(V)
.
∎

The dual of the Cohomological Hall algebra
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}
is a connected vertex bialgebra. Then we have the Theorem

Theorem 7.2.2
.

We have the following

(1)

We have an isomorphism of vertex bialgebras

(96)

\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}\simeq\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q})).

Here, the CoHA coproduct is identified with the coproduct on
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}\simeq\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}))
and the Joyce vertex algebra with the Universal chiral envelope.

(2)

\mathcal{A}_{Q}
is isomorphic to a symmetric algebra of a graded vector space.

Proof.

Part
(1)
follows immediately from the compatibility of the CoHA structure and the Joyce vertex algebra. By Theorem
7.1.1
for connected vertex bialgebras we then immediately get the result.

Using part
(2)
of Theorem
7.1.1
we can conclude that we have an isomorphism of coalgebras

\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}))\simeq\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q})}\simeq\textup{Sym}(\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q})}),

using
[
39
, Thm. 1.3.6]
that the PBW isomorphism
\mathrm{U}\mathfrak{g}\simeq\textup{Sym}(\mathfrak{g})
is an isomorphism of coalgebras for any
g
. Furthermore, in
[
15
, Thm. 5.7, Remark 5.9]
it is shown that

(97)

\mathfrak{g}^{-}_{P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q})}\simeq W[u]

for some graded vector space
W
. Then using Lemma
7.2.1
we get an isomorphism of algebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{Q}\simeq(\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q})))^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq(\textup{Sym}(W[u]))^{\vee}

\displaystyle\simeq\Gamma(W^{*}[u])\qquad\text{(by part 1 of Lemma }\ref{symcoalg})

\displaystyle\simeq\textup{Sym}(W^{*}[u])\qquad\text{(by part 2 of Lemma }\ref{symcoalg})

This proves that
\mathcal{A}_{Q}
is a symmetric algebra.
∎

We note that the above Theorem gives a proof of the integrality conjecture for DT invariants of quivers without potential as well as a proof of Efimov’s theorem that cohomological Hall algebras of symmetric quivers without potential are symmetric algebras.

7.3.
Comparison to Dotsenko-Mozgovoy

In
[
15
]
, the authors also consider vertex algebra structures on
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}
. In particular, they define a vertex Lie algebra
P_{DM}

[
15
, Section 4.4.4]
, in this case the universal chiral envelope
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})
is a certain free vertex algebra. In
[
15
, Thm. 5.6]
the authors give an isomorphism of coalgebras

(98)

\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})\simeq\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}.

This isomorphism then
endows

\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}
with a structure of vertex algebra via
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})
. We now wish to compare the two vertex algebra structures. The key point is that both vertex algebras embed into a lattice vertex algebra given by the Joyce vertex algebra of the homology of the moduli stack of objects of the derived category of quiver representations. By showing that the two subalgebras are the same we get the following theorem

Theorem 7.3.1
.

We have an isomorphism of vertex bialgebras

(99)

\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}\simeq\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})

where
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q}
is equiped with the dual CoHA cocommutative coproduct and the Joyce vertex algebra structure. This isomorphism then induces an isomorphism of primitive vertex Lie algebras

(100)

P(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{Q})\xrightarrow{\simeq}P_{DM}

Using the results of Dotsenko-Mozgovoy we can view the above theorem as a computation in terms of generators and relations of the Joyce vertex bialgebra for any symmetric quiver
Q
.

Lemma 7.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an abelian category. Consider the inclusion
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}
from the moduli of objects of the abelian category to the derived category. Then we have an injection of vertex algebras

(101)

\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}})\to\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}).

Proof.

Firstly, the map is injective since
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}
is an inclusion of a component. The compatibility of vertex algebra structures follows since we have commutative diagrams

(102)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{B}\mathbf{G}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textup{B}\mathbf{G}_{m}\times\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{\textup{D}^{b}\mathcal{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

and the fact that the
Ext
complex restricts to the open component.
∎

We now have the following theorem, which proves that the relation between Joyce vertex algebra for the derived category and Lattice vertex algebras. We remark that originally the theorem works for arbitary quivers but with symmetrised Euler form, however in our case the quiver is symmetric and so there is no need to symmetrise the Euler form and the proof of the original statement goes through word for word.

Theorem 7.3.3
.

[
29
, Thm. 5.19]

There is an isomorphism of graded vertex algebras

(103)

\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\textup{Rep}Q})\simeq L_{\mathbf{Q}^{0}}

for the lattice
\mathbf{Z}^{Q_{0}}
and Euler form
\chi_{Q}
.

Proof of Theorem
7.3.1
.

By
[
15
, Section 4.5.3]
we can view
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})
as a vertex subalgebra of
L_{Q^{0}}\simeq\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{\textup{D}^{b}\textup{Rep}_{Q}})
generated by the elements
e^{i}
. Note that by Theorem
7.3.3
the subvertex algebra

(104)

\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})\xhookrightarrow{}L_{Q^{0}}

contains the elements
e^{i}
. This therefore defines an injective map of graded vertex algebras

(105)

\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})\xrightarrow{}\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})

In
[
15
, Prop. 5.3 ]
it is proven that we have an isomorphism as graded vector spaces

(106)

\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})\simeq\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})

Note that both of our spaces have finite dimensional graded pieces. The statement of
[
15
, Prop. 5.3]
is the dual of equation (
106
) but because the graded pieces are finite dimensional the two statements are equivalent. Equation (
106
) implies that both
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})
and
\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})
have the same graded dimensions. Since the map
\mathrm{U}^{\textup{ch}}(P_{DM})\xrightarrow{}\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\mathcal{M}_{Q})
is injective we can therefore conclude it is an isomorphism.
∎

Appendix A
Cohomology of stacks and characteristic classes of perfect complexes

Throughout this paper, we will work with the derived category of constructible sheaves
\textup{D}(X)
on a stack
X
with the usual
6
-functors and all functors are implicitly derived. We recall that we can define the cohomology of a stack with coefficients in a sheaf
\mathcal{F}

(107)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{F})\ =\ (X\to\textup{pt})_{*}\mathcal{F}

We recover the usual cohomology of
X
by taking
\mathcal{F}=\textbf{{Q}}_{X}
, the constant sheaf.

A.1.
Cohomology

A.1.1.
Künneth formula

The Künneth map

(108)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1}\times X_{2},\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{F}_{2})

is an isomorphism whenever
X_{i}
are Artin stacks locally of finite type and
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})
are graded finite dimensional. Indeed, for such
X_{i}
we have by
[
38
, Thm. 0.1.1]
a Künneth isomorphism for homology groups

\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes\textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})\ \simeq\ \textup{H}_{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1}\times X_{2},\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{F}_{2})

and taking Verdier duals gives (
108
) if the cohomologies of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
are graded finite dimensional.

If the
X_{i}
have infinitely many connected components, (
108
) is usually not an isomorphism, so one must work component-by-component. If we work over a general base
B
, then the relative Künneth map

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes_{\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(B)}\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1}\times_{B}X_{2},\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{F}_{2})

is often not an isomorphism;
[
38
]
only supplies an isomorphism of sheaves on
B
.

A.1.2.
Cup products

The
cup product
map on two sheaves is the map

\cup\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{F})\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{G})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X\times X,\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\mathcal{G})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})

composing the Künneth map with pullback along the diagonal
\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\mathcal{G}\to\Delta_{*}\Delta^{*}(\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}
. We often denote it by
\cdot
. If
f:Y\to X
is a map of Artin stacks, it is easy to show that

(109)

f^{*}(\alpha\cup\beta)\ =\ f^{*}(\alpha)\cup f^{*}(\beta)

for any cohomology classes
\alpha,\beta
of
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
.

Since
\textbf{{Q}}_{X}
is the unit for
\otimes
, it follows that
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X)
is a supercommutative algebra and acts on the cohomology of every sheaf. If
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
is any map of sheaves, the induced map

(110)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{F})\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{G})

is
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X)
-linear, and

Lemma A.1.3
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of stacks. Then we have an isomorphism of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y)
-modules

(111)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{F})\simeq\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y,f_{*}\mathcal{F})

where the action on the left is via
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y)\to\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X)
.

Proof.

Note that the sheaf theoretic projection formula gives a natural isomorphism

(112)

-\otimes f_{*}\mathcal{G}\ \mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\ f_{*}(f^{*}-\otimes\mathcal{G})

which we apply to a morphism
\alpha:\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}\to\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}
to get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}\otimes f_{*}\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}(f^{*}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}(\textbf{{Q}}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}\otimes f_{*}\mathcal{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}(f^{*}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{*}(\textbf{{Q}}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{G})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\otimes\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\alpha\otimes\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\alpha\otimes\textup{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

from which it follows that there is an isomorphism
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y,f_{*}\mathcal{G})=\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\mathcal{G})
of
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y)
-modules.
∎

A.1.4.
Vanishing cycles

If we have a function
f:X\to\textbf{{A}}^{1}
, we may define the
vanishing cycles
functor

\varphi_{f}\ :\ \textup{D}(X)\ \to\ \textup{D}(X).

It satisfies the following properties:

(1)

(Thom–Sebastiani). If
X_{i}
are smooth with functions
f_{i}
, then there is an isomorphism

(113)

\textup{TS}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1},\varphi_{f_{1}}\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{2},\varphi_{f_{2}}\mathcal{F}_{2})\ \simeq\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X_{1}\times X_{2},\varphi_{f_{1}\boxplus f_{2}}\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{F}_{2})

(2)

(Push and pull). If
g:X\to Y
is a map of smooth stacks and
f
a function on
Y
, then there are natural transformations

\displaystyle\varphi_{f}

\displaystyle\ \to\ g_{*}\varphi_{g^{*}f}g^{*}

\displaystyle g_{!}\varphi_{fg}g^{!}\textbf{{D}}

\displaystyle\ \to\ \varphi_{f}\textbf{{D}}

which induce maps on cohomology (for the second, assuming that
g
is proper):

(114)

\displaystyle g^{*}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y,\varphi_{f}\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\varphi_{g^{*}f}\mathcal{F}).

(115)

\displaystyle g_{*}\ :\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\varphi_{g^{*}f}\textbf{{Q}}_{X})

\displaystyle\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y,\varphi_{f}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y})[\delta]

where
\delta=2\dim Y-2\dim X
and the pushforward map is the composition

(116)

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\varphi_{gf}\textbf{{Q}}_{X})\ =\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y,g_{*}\varphi_{gf}g^{*}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y})\ =\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y,g_{!}\varphi_{gf}g^{!}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y})[\delta]\ \to\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y,\varphi_{f}\textbf{{Q}}_{X})[\delta].

Here the second equivalence uses properness of
g
to deduce
g_{!}=g_{*}
and smoothness of the spaces to get that
g^{!}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}[\delta]=g^{*}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}
.

(3)

(Projection formula) If
g
as above is proper, then we have

(117)

g_{*}(g^{*}(\alpha)\cup\beta)\ =\ \alpha\cup g_{*}(\beta)

for any cohomology class
\alpha\in\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(Y)
and critical cohomology class
\beta\in\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X,\varphi_{fg}\textbf{{Q}}_{X})
.

To show the projection formula, we note that the first equality in (
116
) intertwines the actions of
f^{*}(\alpha)
and
\alpha
by Lemma
A.1.3
. The second and third map are induced by maps of sheaves

g_{*}\varphi_{gf}g^{*}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}\ \simeq\ g_{!}\varphi_{gf}g^{!}\textbf{{Q}}_{Y}[\delta]\ \to\ \varphi_{f}\textbf{{Q}}_{X}[\delta]

which intertwines the action of
\alpha
on both sides by the linearity of the map (
110
) over action of cohomology by cup product.

A.2.
Chern classes for perfect complexes

A.2.1.
Tautological bundles

We have the stacks

\textup{BGL}\ =\ \bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\textup{BGL}_{n},\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{Perf}\ =\ \bigsqcup_{n\in\textbf{{Z}}}\textup{Perf}_{n}

whose
S
-points are rank
n
vector bundles and rank
n
perfect complexes on
S
respectively. In particular, both carry a tautological vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
and perfect complex
\mathcal{F}
respectively, which have rank
n
on the
n
th component.

A.2.2.

Define a locally constant function
rk
on
BGL
or
Perf
, which on a given component
\textup{BGL}_{n}
or
\textup{Perf}_{n}
is the constant function
n
. The
rank

\textup{rk}(V)
of a vector bundle or perfect complex
V
is the locally constant function on
X
given by pulling back
rk
via the classifying map
X\to\textup{BGL}
or
X\to\textup{Perf}
.

A.2.3.

We denote the tautological line bundle on
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}=\textup{BGL}_{1}
by
\gamma
. We define the tensor product and direct sum maps

\displaystyle\otimes\ :\ \textup{BGL}\times\textup{BGL}\ \to\ \textup{BGL},\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\ :\ \textup{Perf}\times\textup{Perf}\ \to\ \textup{Perf}

\displaystyle\oplus\ :\ \textup{BGL}\times\textup{BGL}\ \to\ \textup{BGL},\hskip 42.67912pt\oplus\ :\ \textup{Perf}\times\textup{Perf}\ \to\ \textup{Perf}

by defining
\otimes^{*}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}
and
\oplus^{*}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}\boxplus\mathcal{E}
and similarly for
\mathcal{F}
. In particular, we have maps

\displaystyle\textup{act}\ :\ \textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\textup{BGL}\ \to\ \textup{BGL},\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{act}\ :\ \textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times\textup{Perf}\ \to\ \textup{Perf}

defined by
\textup{act}^{*}\mathcal{E}=\gamma\boxtimes\mathcal{E}
and similarly for
\mathcal{F}
. We also have the maps

\vee\ :\ \textup{BGL}\ \to\ \textup{BGL},\hskip 42.67912pt\vee\ :\ \textup{Perf}\ \to\ \textup{Perf}

defined by
\vee^{*}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
and similarly for
\mathcal{F}
.

A.2.4.
Chern classes

We have isomorphisms

\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{BGL}_{n})\ \simeq\ \textbf{{Q}}[c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}],\hskip 42.67912pt\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{Perf}_{n})\ \simeq\ \textbf{{Q}}[c_{1},c_{2},\ldots\ ]

where
c_{i}
have cohomological degree
2i
and satisfy

(118)

\oplus^{*}c_{n}\ =\ c_{n}\otimes 1+c_{n-1}\otimes c_{1}+\cdots+1\otimes c_{n}.

It was shown by Grothendieck in the vector bundle case that this property characterises the
c_{i}
uniquely, up to multiplying
c_{1}
by a nonzero scalar.

For any vector bundle or perfect complex
V
, we define the
chern classes

c_{i}(V)
by pullback of the generators
c_{i}
along the classifying map
X\to\textup{BGL}
or
X\to\textup{Perf}
, and the
chern series
by

c(V,z)\ =\ \sum_{r\geq 0}c_{r}(V,z)z^{r}

where
c_{0}=1
. Moreover,

Proposition A.2.5
.

The chern series satisfies

(1)

f^{*}c(V,z)=c(f^{*}V,z)
for any map
f:X\to Y
and vector bundle or perfect complex
V
on
Y
.

(2)

c(V_{1}\oplus V_{2},z)\ =\ c(V_{1},z)\cdot c(V_{2},z)
for vector bundles or perfect complexes
V_{1},V_{2}
.

(3)

c(V\otimes\mathcal{L},z^{-1})=\left(\frac{z+c_{1}(\mathcal{L})}{z}\right)^{\textup{rk}\mathcal{V}}c(V,(z+c_{1}(\mathcal{L}))^{-1})
for line bundle
\mathcal{L}
, i.e. the left hand side is the expansion as a power series in
z^{-1}
of the right side.

(4)

c(V^{\vee},z)=c(V,-z)
.

Part (3) can be proven by expanding the chern series as an exponential of chern characters, see
[
34
, Lem. 2.6.19]
.

A.2.6.
Euler classes

If
V
is a vector bundle of rank
n
, its
Euler class
is its top chern class
e(V)=c_{n}(V)
. Likewise, given a perfect complex given by a complex of vector bundles

V\ =\ \left(V_{-n}\to V_{-n+1}\to\cdots\to V_{m-1}\to V_{m}\right)

we define its
Euler class
as

e(V)\ =\ e(V_{n})^{\pm}\cdots\frac{1}{e(V_{-1})}\cdot e(V_{0})\cdot\frac{1}{e(V_{1})}\cdots e(V_{m})^{\pm}\ \in\ \textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X)[e(V_{i})^{-1}\ :\ i\textup{ odd}].

A.2.7.
The generating series
\Psi

We define for any perfect complex or vector bundle
V
the power series

(119)

\Psi(V,z)=z^{\textup{rk}V}c(V,z^{-1})

and from the properties of the chern series in Proposition
A.2.5
it follows that

Lemma A.2.8
.

We have that

(1)

f^{*}\Psi(V,z)=\Psi(f^{*}V,z)
for any map
f:X\to Y
and vector bundle or perfect complex
V
on
Y
.

(2)

\Psi(V_{1}\oplus V_{2},z)=\Psi(V_{1},z)\cdot\Psi(V_{2},z)
.

(3)

\Psi(V\otimes\mathcal{L},z)=\Psi(V,z+c_{1}(\mathcal{L}))
for line bundle
\mathcal{L}
, i.e. the left hand side is the expansion as a Laurent series in
z
of the right side.

(4)

\Psi(V^{\vee},z)=(-1)^{\textup{rk}V}\Psi(V,-z)
.

A.2.9.
Chern roots

Given a vector bundle
V
on
X
induced by
X\to\textup{BGL}_{n}
, its
chern roots
are the images
x_{V,1},\ldots,x_{V,n}\in\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X\times_{\textup{BGL}_{n}}\textup{B}T)
of the generators of
\textup{H}^{2}(\textup{B}T)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{\textup{BGL}_{n}}\textup{B}T_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{B}T_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{BGL}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}

where
T_{n}
is the maximal torus of diagonal elements inside
\textup{GL}_{n}
. In the setting in the body of the paper,
X=\mathcal{M}_{Q,d}^{T}
and the pullback
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X)\hookrightarrow\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(X\times_{\textup{BGL}_{n}}\textup{B}T_{n})
is an injection, so for computations in
X
it suffices to compute in terms of chern roots, for instance we have

c(V,z)\ =\ (1+zx_{V,1})\cdots(1+zx_{V,n})

and therefore

\Psi(V,z)\ =\ (z+x_{V,1})\cdots(z+x_{V,n}),\hskip 42.67912pte(V)\ =\ x_{V,1}\cdots x_{V,n}.

Proposition A.2.10
.

For
V
a vector bundle or bounded complex of vector bundles on
Y
and
n\neq 0
, we have that

\Psi(V,nz)\ =\ e(\gamma^{n}\boxtimes V)

where
\gamma
is the tautological line bundle on
\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}
, and we expand the right hand side–a priori a localised cohomology class in
\textup{H}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.75}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}}}(\textup{B}\textup{{G}}_{m}\times Y)
–as a Laurent series in
z^{-1}=c_{1}(\gamma)^{-1}
.

Proof.

By multiplicativity of Euler classes and
\Psi(-,z)
over direct sums of perfect complexes by the Lemma
A.2.8
, it suffices to prove this for each factor of
V
, i.e. assume that
V
is a vector bundle. The chern roots of
\gamma^{n}\boxtimes V
are
x_{i}+c_{1}(\gamma^{n})=x_{i}+nz
where
x_{i}
are the chern roots of
V
, and so we have

e(\gamma^{n}\boxtimes V)\ =\ \bigsqcap_{i}(x_{i}+nz)\ =\ \Psi(V,nz),

proving the Proposition.
∎

A.2.11.
Example

For instance, the chern series, Euler class and
\Psi
series of a two term perfect complex
V=(V_{0}\to V_{1})
is

(120)

c(V,z)=\frac{c(V_{0},z)}{c(V_{1},z)},\hskip 42.67912pte(V)\ =\ \frac{e(V_{0})}{e(V_{1})},\hskip 42.67912pt\Psi(V,z)\ =\ \frac{\Psi(V_{0},z)}{\Psi(V_{1},z)}.

If the chern roots of
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
are denoted by
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
, these are

(121)

c(V,z)=\frac{\bigsqcap(1+zx_{i})}{\bigsqcap(1+zy_{j})},\hskip 42.67912pte(V)\ =\ \frac{\bigsqcap x_{i}}{\bigsqcap y_{j}},\hskip 42.67912pt\Psi(V,z)\ =\ \frac{\bigsqcap(z+x_{i})}{\bigsqcap(z+y_{j})}.
